7
Case study: Heterogeneity by preferences

7.1
Economic research question

In the past forty years, centralized school assignment mechanisms have proliferated, alongside research on alternatives to traditional neighborhood-based education in the United States. A thorough investigation of these mechanisms led many districts to adopt variants of the classical Deferred Acceptance (DA) or Random Serial Dictatorship (RSD) mechanisms
(
Abdulkadiroğlu and Sönmez, 2003
;
Abdulkadiroğlu et al., 2005
;
Pathak and Sönmez, 2008
)
. Students submit rank-ordered lists of schools to a central authority, who uses the lists to assign schools. These mechanisms are strategy-proof: under rational behavior, students truthfully report their preferences.

Centralized assignment, and school choice reforms more broadly (e.g. vouchers), are nonetheless controversial. Learning and information frictions may limit the benefits of choice
(
Narita, 2018
)
, and choice reforms may have nuanced supply-side effects
(
Bau, 2022
)
.
An important question in this literature concerns match effects: is there systematic heterogeneity in learning quality across students at a given school, and, if so, is this heterogeneity reflected in student preferences? The answer has implications for the potential welfare gains from choice, and the extent to which they are realized.

Prior approaches are somewhat indirect: some examples are (i) testing the heterogeneity explained by the gap between a student’s baseline achievement level and the school’s median level
(
Angrist et al., 2023
)
, or (ii) estimating structural models of demand to explain student preferences in terms of observable quantities
(
Bau, 2022
;
Narita, 2018
)
.
We instead pursue a direct approach, using KRR inference to model and test for heterogeneity in school effects depending upon reported preferences. Building on
Abdulkadiroğlu et al., 2017
, we use unified enrollment lotteries to identify school effects conditional upon reported preferences. Thus, it is possible to study directly whether students’ ranked preferences explain heterogeneity in school effects, without estimating an underlying choice model. Because KRR exploits latent, low-dimensional structure in ranking data despite their dimensionality, and because we provide new methods for inference, this direct approach becomes tractable.

7.2
Semi-synthetic preference data

We generate synthetic student ranked preference lists for Boston Public School students by combining publicly available data sets. Though the underlying student-level micro data are not publicly available,
Pathak and Shi, 2021
report coefficients from models estimated using real preference data.
Pathak and Shi, 2021
show that their model reproduces the distribution of student preferences in subsequent years with reasonable accuracy, motivating this procedure.

We generate each student ranked preference list as follows. First, we draw a location and covariate vector for that student from the distribution represented in the American Community Survey (ACS). We also obtain school locations and other covariates from the Massachusetts Department of Elementary and Secondary Education (DESE). Next, we compute walking distances from that student’s location to various schools, using the Google Maps API for distances. We then use the random utility model of
Pathak and Shi, 2021
to generate the preference list. See Appendix
L
for details. We use these preference lists and the standard RSD mechanism to assign students to schools.

7.3
A kernel for rankings

The data consist of 4000 synthetic students’ rankings of 25 Boston high-schools, so the ambient dimension is larger than
10^{25}
. For this reason, the direct approach is intractable with standard methods. We now describe how our KRR inference procedure makes the direct approach tractable, i.e. relatively efficient to implement and adaptive to latent, low-dimensional structure in student preferences. In particular, we demonstrate that our inferential procedure detects heterogeneity with non-trivial power.

Previous work shows the effectiveness of KRR for ranking data
(
Kondor and Barbosa, 2010
;
Jiao and Vert, 2015
;
Mania et al., 2018
)
, though without the uniform inference guarantees necessary to test for match effects. Following
Mania et al., 2018
, in our implementation we use the Mallows kernel for ranking data.

Definition 7.1

(Mallows kernel;
Mania et al., 2018
)
.

Given a set of
q
alternatives
\mathcal{A}
and two complete ranked preference lists
\pi,\pi^{\prime}:\mathcal{A}\to\left\{1,\ldots,q\right\}
, the number of discordant pairs is

N(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\coloneqq\#\left\{(s,t)\in\mathcal{A}\times\mathcal{A}\,\middle|\,\pi^{\prime}(s)>\pi(s),\pi^{\prime}(t)<\pi(t)\right\}.
It is the number of unordered pairs
\{s,t\}
such that
\pi
prefers
s
to
t
but
\pi^{\prime}
does not, or vice versa. The Mallows kernel is
k(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\coloneqq\exp\{-\ell N(\pi,\pi^{\prime})\}
, where
\ell
is called the length-scale.

Intuitively, two preference lists are similar if they make similar pairwise comparisons. The following result justifies this kernel choice. It implies that the RKHS for the Mallows kernel is automatically well-specified.

Lemma 7.2

(Universality;
Mania et al., 2018
, Theorem 5
)
.

The Mallows kernel is universal. Formally, if
\mu
is a probability distribution over the set of rank-ordered lists, then the RKHS for the Mallows kernel contains all functions

\mathrm{supp}(\mu)\to\mathbb{R}
.

Remark 7.3

(Universality)
.

Conventionally, a kernel is universal if and only if
H
is dense in
L^{2}(\mu)
. In the case of rankings, this stronger statement is possible because the set of rank-ordered lists is finite.

7.4
Do students who highly rank schools benefit more?

Finally, we use our inferential procedure to detect heterogeneity in school sector effects. Under the RSD lottery, sector assignment is random conditional upon a student’s ranked preference list
(
Abdulkadiroğlu et al., 2017
)
. Moreover, the treatment propensity is determined completely by the RSD lottery, and may be computed to arbitrary precision by running the RSD lottery many times. Thus, conditional school-sector effects are non-parametrically identified as a function of student preferences.

We use KRR inference to distinguish between two counterfactual scenarios: no match effects versus match effects. In the no match effect scenario, we generate student preferences as described above. We draw school effects as described in Appendix
L
. Here, student taste parameters, which correspond to random coefficients from the model of
Pathak and Shi, 2021
, do not incorporate information about student-specific match effects. In the match effect scenario, we modify student preferences to incorporate a random, latent taste parameter
\chi_{i}
which represents a preference for pilot sector schools. This taste parameter also determines sector effects, so that students who prefer pilot sector schools also benefit more from them. See Appendix
L
for details.

To begin, Figure
4
verifies our key assumption that the local width is small. Using the student rank lists generated by the mixed logit model of
Pathak and Shi, 2021
, and using the Mallows kernel, we calculate the kernel matrix
K
as described in Section
3
. Following
Mania et al., 2018
, we set the hyperparameter
\ell
equal to the median number of discordant pairs. We plot the top
50
eigenvalues of
K
. After the top
25
eigenvalues, corresponding to the number of schools, the remaining eigenvalues are negligible. Our arguments based upon local width exploit this fact.

Figure 4
:
KRR exploits latent low-dimensional structure in student rankings.

To estimate the conditional average treatment effect function, given that the treatment propensity score is exactly known, we regress the inverse propensity weighted outcome

\tilde{Y}_{i}=\frac{Y_{i}D_{i}}{p(X_{i})}-\frac{Y_{i}(1-D_{i})}{1-p(X_{i})}
on student reported rankings
X_{i}
using KRR. This provides an unbiased signal of the conditional average treatment effect, which is
\gamma_{1}
in the former scenario or
\gamma_{1}+\gamma_{2}\mathbb{E}[\chi_{i}|X_{i}]
in the latter scenario, where
\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. The simplicity of this function loosely corresponds to our assumption that
f_{0}
is smooth.

To perform interpretable inference, we divide student preference lists into groups
S_{\rho}
, where
\rho
is the highest ranking assigned to a pilot school in a student’s preference list.
We then perform simultaneous inference on the full vector of group averages, using our theoretical results to study the statistic
F(\hat{f}-f_{0})=\max_{1\leq\rho\leq 25}\mathbb{E}_{n}\{(\hat{f}-f_{0})(X_{i})|X_{i}\in S_{\rho}\}
, which is continuous with respect to the
\sup
norm.

Figure
5
demonstrates that our KRR inference procedure successfully distinguishes between the two scenarios. We display group average treatment effects, where groups are defined by the highest rank given to a pilot school. Light blue bars are simultaneous confidence bands obtained using our procedure, and red lines denote the true group averages. In both scenarios, we cover the truth. In the match effect scenario, we reject the null hypothesis of no effect for students who highly rank pilot schools.

Interestingly, the average treatment effect is not identified by simple group averages, since the treatment propensity is non-constant within the group for
\rho>1
. Therefore comparing treatment and control means directly within rank strata is not sufficient to identify heterogeneity in this experiment.
20
20

20

Group average treatment effects, as reported above, are identified by averaging inverse propensity weighted outcomes within rank strata. We find in Appendix
L
that this method is not precise enough to detect heterogeneity; KRR dramatically improves precision by conditioning upon the full rank list.
See Appendix
L
for further discussion.

(a)

No match effects.

(b)

Match effects.

Figure 5
:
KRR inference detects match effects.

8
Conclusion

The increasing availability of truthfully reported preference data, as well as other complex types of economic data, motivates us to study KRR inference. Theoretically, our goal is to prove uniform inference guarantees; practically, our goal is to create new tools for economic data. We propose new uniform confidence bands that have simple closed form solutions, strong statistical guarantees, and robust empirical performance
across nonlinear settings. While Section
6
focuses on KRR, Section
5
builds a framework to use Gaussian and bootstrap couplings for more general kernel methods. Future research may extend our results to non-parametric estimands beyond regression.

Appendix B
Bahadur representation

The sketch in Section
6
has the step

\displaystyle\sqrt{n}(\hat{f}-f_{\lambda})\approx\sqrt{n}T_{\lambda}^{-1}\{(\hat{T}-T)(f_{0}-f_{\lambda})+\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]\}=\sqrt{n}\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}].

We prove this approximation in what follows.

B.1
First order lemmas

Lemma B.1

(Higher-order resolvent)
.

Let
V
be a vector space and
A,B:V\to V
be invertible linear operators. Then, for all
l\geq 1
, it holds

{A}^{-1}-{B}^{-1}=A^{-1}\{(B-A)B^{-1}\}^{l}+\sum_{r=1}^{l-1}B^{-1}\{(B-A)B^{-1}\}^{r}.

Proof.

Note that when
l=1
this reduces to the familiar “resolvent identity”

A^{-1}-B^{-1}=A^{-1}(B-A)B^{-1}\iff A^{-1}=A^{-1}(B-A)B^{-1}+B^{-1}.

We then prove by induction. Supposing the inequality holds for
l-1
, we have

\displaystyle\quad{A}^{-1}-{B}^{-1}

\displaystyle=A^{-1}\{(B-A)B^{-1}\}^{l-1}+\sum_{r=1}^{l-2}B^{-1}\{(B-A)B^{-1}\}^{r}.

Plugging in the resolvent identity again for the left-most appearance of
A^{-1}
gives

\displaystyle=\{A^{-1}(B-A)B^{-1}+B^{-1}\}\{(B-A)B^{-1}\}^{l-1}+\sum_{r=1}^{l-2}B^{-1}\{(B-A)B^{-1}\}^{r}

\displaystyle=A^{-1}\{(B-A)B^{-1}\}^{l}+B^{-1}\{(B-A)B^{-1}\}^{l-1}+\sum_{r=1}^{l-2}B^{-1}\{(B-A)B^{-1}\}^{r}

\displaystyle=A^{-1}\{(B-A)B^{-1}\}^{l}+\sum_{r=1}^{l-1}B^{-1}\{(B-A)B^{-1}\}^{r}.

This proves the inductive hypothesis.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Suppose
\|T_{\lambda}^{-1}(\hat{T}-T)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\delta<1
.
Then for all
k\geq 1

(\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}-T_{\lambda}^{-1})u=A_{1}u+A_{2}T_{\lambda}^{-1}u+A_{3}T^{-1}_{\lambda}u

where

\|A_{1}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\frac{\delta^{k}}{\lambda},\quad\|A_{2}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\delta,\quad\|A_{3}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{1-\delta}.

Proof.

By applying Lemma
B.1
with
A=\hat{T}_{\lambda}=\hat{T}+\lambda
and
B=T_{\lambda}=T+\lambda
, we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}-T_{\lambda}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}\{(T-\hat{T})T_{\lambda}^{-1}\}^{k}+\sum_{r=1}^{k-1}T_{\lambda}^{-1}\{(T-\hat{T})T_{\lambda}^{-1}\}^{r}

\displaystyle=\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}\{(T-\hat{T})T_{\lambda}^{-1}\}^{k}+T_{\lambda}^{-1}(T-\hat{T})T_{\lambda}^{-1}+T_{\lambda}^{-1}\sum_{r=2}^{k-1}\{(T-\hat{T})T_{\lambda}^{-1}\}^{r}

\displaystyle=\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}\{(T-\hat{T})T_{\lambda}^{-1}\}^{k}+T_{\lambda}^{-1}(T-\hat{T})T_{\lambda}^{-1}+\left[\sum_{r=2}^{k-1}\{T_{\lambda}^{-1}(T-\hat{T})\}^{r}\right]T_{\lambda}^{-1}

\displaystyle\eqqcolon A_{1}+A_{2}T_{\lambda}^{-1}+A_{3}T_{\lambda}^{-1},

where in the third equality we’ve used that
B(AB)^{r}=(BA)^{r}B
.
Since
\|(T-\hat{T})T_{\lambda}^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\delta<1
,

\|A_{1}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}=\|\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}\{(T-\hat{T})T_{\lambda}^{-1}\}^{k}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\frac{\delta^{k}}{\lambda},\quad\|A_{2}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}=\|T_{\lambda}^{-1}(T-\hat{T})\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\delta,

where in the first inequality we used that
\left\lVert AB\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{op}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\left\lVert B\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}
. Using this again along with the triangle inequality for
\left\lVert-\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}
, we find

\displaystyle\|A_{3}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{r=2}^{k-1}\{T_{\lambda}^{-1}(T-\hat{T})\}^{r}\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\sum_{r=2}^{k-1}\left\|\{T_{\lambda}^{-1}(T-\hat{T})\}\right\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{r}\leq\sum_{r=2}^{k-1}\delta^{r}\leq\sum_{r=2}^{\infty}\delta^{r}=\frac{\delta^{2}}{1-\delta}.

∎

B.2
Main result

Proposition B.3
.

Suppose
\|T_{\lambda}^{-1}(\hat{T}-T)\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\delta\leq\frac{1}{2}
, and
\left\lVert T_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]\right\rVert\leq\gamma
. Then

\hat{f}-f_{\lambda}=T_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]+T_{\lambda}^{-1}(\hat{T}-T)(f_{0}-f_{\lambda})+u

(2)

for some
u
with

\left\lVert u\right\rVert\leq\frac{\delta}{1-\delta}\left(\gamma+\delta\|f_{0}-f_{\lambda}\|\right)\leq 2\delta\left(\gamma+\delta\|f_{0}-f_{\lambda}\|\right).

Proof.

We proceed in steps.

1.

Decomposition. Write

\displaystyle\hat{f}-f_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}Y_{i}]-T^{-1}_{\lambda}Tf_{0}=\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}\hat{T}f_{0}+\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]-T^{-1}_{\lambda}Tf_{0}.

Adding and subracting the terms
T^{-1}_{\lambda}\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]
and
T^{-1}_{\lambda}\hat{T}f_{0}-T^{-1}_{\lambda}Tf_{0}
, this is

\displaystyle=(\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}-T^{-1}_{\lambda})\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]+T^{-1}_{\lambda}\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]

\displaystyle\qquad+(\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}-T^{-1}_{\lambda})\hat{T}f_{0}+T_{\lambda}^{-1}\hat{T}f_{0}-T^{-1}_{\lambda}Tf_{0}

\displaystyle=(\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}-T^{-1}_{\lambda})\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]+T^{-1}_{\lambda}\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]

\displaystyle\qquad+(\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}-T^{-1}_{\lambda})(\hat{T}-T)f_{0}+(\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}-T^{-1}_{\lambda})Tf_{0}+T_{\lambda}^{-1}\hat{T}f_{0}-T^{-1}_{\lambda}Tf_{0}

\displaystyle=(\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}-T^{-1}_{\lambda})\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]

\displaystyle\qquad+(\hat{T}-T)f_{0}\}+T^{-1}_{\lambda}\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]

\displaystyle\qquad-\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\hat{T}-T)f_{\lambda}+T_{\lambda}^{-1}(\hat{T}-T)f_{0}

where we used the transformation

\displaystyle(\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}-T^{-1}_{\lambda})Tf_{0}=\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}(T-\hat{T})T_{\lambda}^{-1}Tf_{0}=\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}(T-\hat{T})f_{\lambda}=-\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\hat{T}-T)f_{\lambda}.

Focusing on the final two terms

\displaystyle-\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\hat{T}-T)f_{\lambda}+T_{\lambda}^{-1}(\hat{T}-T)f_{0}

\displaystyle=-\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}(\hat{T}-T)f_{\lambda}+T_{\lambda}^{-1}(\hat{T}-T)f_{0}\pm T^{-1}_{\lambda}(\hat{T}-T)f_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=(T^{-1}_{\lambda}-\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda})(\hat{T}-T)f_{\lambda}+T_{\lambda}^{-1}(\hat{T}-T)(f_{0}-f_{\lambda})

\displaystyle=(\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}-T^{-1}_{\lambda})(\hat{T}-T)(-f_{\lambda})+T_{\lambda}^{-1}(\hat{T}-T)(f_{0}-f_{\lambda}).

In summary,

\displaystyle\hat{f}-f_{\lambda}

\displaystyle=(\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}-T^{-1}_{\lambda})\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]+(\hat{T}-T)(f_{0}-f_{\lambda})\}

\displaystyle\qquad+T^{-1}_{\lambda}\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]+T_{\lambda}^{-1}(\hat{T}-T)(f_{0}-f_{\lambda})

i.e.

\hat{f}-f_{\lambda}=(\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}-T^{-1}_{\lambda})S_{n}+T^{-1}_{\lambda}S_{n},\quad S_{n}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]+(\hat{T}-T)(f_{0}-f_{\lambda}).

2.

What remains is to control the first term:

(\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}-T^{-1}_{\lambda})S_{n},\quad S_{n}\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{n}[k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]+(\hat{T}-T)(f_{0}-f_{\lambda}).

Recall Lemma
B.2
:

(\hat{T}_{\lambda}^{-1}-T_{\lambda}^{-1})u=A_{1}u+A_{2}T_{\lambda}^{-1}u+A_{3}T^{-1}_{\lambda}u

where

\|A_{1}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\frac{\delta^{k}}{\lambda},\quad\|A_{2}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\delta,\quad\|A_{3}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\frac{\delta^{2}}{1-\delta}.

Therefore

\displaystyle\|(\hat{T}^{-1}_{\lambda}-T^{-1}_{\lambda})S_{n}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|A_{1}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\cdot\|S_{n}\|+\|A_{2}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\cdot\|T_{\lambda}^{-1}S_{n}\|+\|A_{3}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\cdot\|T_{\lambda}^{-1}S_{n}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta^{k}}{\lambda}\|S_{n}\|+\left(\delta+\frac{\delta^{2}}{1-\delta}\right)\|T_{\lambda}^{-1}S_{n}\|

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta^{k}}{\lambda}\|S_{n}\|+\frac{\delta}{1-\delta}\|T_{\lambda}^{-1}S_{n}\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\delta^{k}}{\lambda}\|S_{n}\|+\frac{\delta}{1-\delta}\left(\gamma+\delta\|f_{0}-f_{\lambda}\|\right).

Taking
k\uparrow\infty
removes the first term, which finishes the proof.

∎

Theorem B.4
.

Suppose that
n\geq 16\kappa^{2}\ln(4/\eta)^{2}[\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)\vee\lambda^{-1}]
. It then holds with probability
1-\eta
that

\hat{f}-f_{\lambda}=\mathbb{E}_{n}[T_{\lambda}^{-1}k_{X_{i}}\varepsilon_{i}]+T_{\lambda}^{-1}(\hat{T}-T)(f_{0}-f_{\lambda})+u

for some
u
with

\left\lVert u\right\rVert\leq 8(\kappa^{2}\left\lVert f_{0}-f_{\lambda}\right\rVert+\bar{\sigma}\kappa)\ln(4/\eta)^{2}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)}{n}}\vee\frac{2\kappa}{n\lambda}\right\}^{2}.

Proof.

By combining Lemmas
H.4
and
H.5
with a union bound, we have with probability at least
1-\eta
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}T_{\lambda}^{-1}\varepsilon_{i}k_{X_{i}}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 2\bar{\sigma}\ln(4/\eta)\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)}{n}}\vee\frac{2\kappa}{n\lambda}\right\}=\gamma

\displaystyle\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}T_{\lambda}^{-1}(T_{i}-T)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\kappa\ln(4/\eta)\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)}{n}}\vee\frac{2\kappa}{n\lambda}\right\}=\delta.

On the event that these inequalities hold, and provided that
\delta<\frac{1}{2}
, we have from Proposition
B.3
that (
2
) holds with

\left\lVert u\right\rVert\leq 8(\kappa^{2}\left\lVert f_{0}\right\rVert+\bar{\sigma}\kappa)\ln(4/\eta)^{2}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)}{n}}\vee\frac{2\kappa}{n\lambda}\right\}^{2}.

Moreover, the condition
\delta\leq\frac{1}{2}
can be seen to hold whenever

n\geq 16\kappa^{2}\ln(4/\eta)^{2}\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)\vee 8\kappa^{2}\lambda^{-1}\ln(4/\eta).

In particular, consider the two cases

2\kappa\ln(4/\eta)\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)}{n}}<\frac{1}{2}\iff 4\kappa\ln(4/\eta)\sqrt{\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)}<\sqrt{n}\iff 16\kappa^{2}\ln(4/\eta)^{2}\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)<n

and

2\kappa\ln(4/\eta)\frac{2\kappa}{n\lambda}<\frac{1}{2}\iff 8\kappa^{2}\ln(4/\eta)\frac{1}{\lambda}<n.

Since
\eta\in[0,1]
,
4/\eta\geq e
and so
\ln(4/\eta)^{2}\geq\ln(4/\eta)
. Thus, we see that the condition
n\geq 16\kappa^{2}\ln(4/\eta)^{2}[\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)\vee\lambda^{-1}]
is sufficient.
∎

Appendix C
Gaussian couplings

The sketch in Section
6
claims a high-probability bound on

\left\lVert\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\right)-Z\right\rVert,

where
Z
is a Gaussian random variable in
H
with covariance
\Sigma=\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]
and

U_{i}=T_{\lambda}^{-1}\{(k_{X_{i}}\otimes k_{X_{i}}^{*}-T)(f_{0}-f_{\lambda})+\varepsilon_{i}k_{X_{i}}\}.

We prove this approximation in what follows, via results for general summands
U_{i}
.

C.1
Notation and preliminaries

Let
U=(U_{1},U_{2},\ldots)
be an i.i.d. sequence of centered random elements in
H
. Let
\Sigma:H\to H
denote the covariance operator associated to the summands
U_{i}
, so that for all
u,v\in H
and
i\geq 1
,

\mathbb{E}[\left\langle u,\,U_{i}\right\rangle\left\langle v,\,U_{i}\right\rangle]=\left\langle u,\,\Sigma v\right\rangle.
Let us also suppose that
\mathbb{E}\left\lVert U_{i}\right\rVert^{2}<\infty
. Then
\Sigma
is trace-class and self-adjoint, so that we may choose an
H
-orthonormal basis of
\Sigma
-eigenvectors
(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots)
, with corresponding eigenvalues
(\nu_{1},\nu_{2},\ldots)
. We also define the numbers

\sigma^{2}(m):=\sigma^{2}(\Sigma,m)=\sum_{k>m}\nu_{k},
which play a role similar to the metric entropy, giving a quantitative measure of the compactness of
\Sigma
and hence the tightness of
\mu
. Given some
m
, let

\Pi_{m}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}^{*}

be the self-adjoint and idempotent projection onto the span of
(e_{1},e_{2},\ldots,e_{m})
.

In this section, our aim will be to construct a Gaussian process
x\mapsto Z(x)
indexed by
x\in S
such that the quantity

\sup_{x\in S}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}(x)-Z(x)\right|

(3)

is bounded with high probability in terms of
n
and
\Sigma
; in other words, we wish to construct nonasymptotic Gaussian couplings for RKHS-valued partial sums in
L^{\infty}
.

C.1.1
Coupling technicalities

In order to construct couplings, we require that the background probability space
(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F},\Omega)
is sufficiently rich. For example, it is enough to assume that there exists a countable sequence of standard normal random variable independent of
U
. This is a minor technicality; ultimately, the couplings in this paper are a logical device used to quantify similarity of distributions and establish validity of inference procedures.

C.2
Discussion

The nonasymptotic nature of our bounds is crucial in inverse problems such as kernel ridge regression, where the complexity of
\Sigma
increases with
n
, and thus the limiting process is non-Donsker.
Moreover, the bounds we derive are
universal
in that they only depend on
n
, the spectrum of
\Sigma
, and boundedness of the kernel:

\sup_{x\in S}\left\lVert k_{x}\right\rVert=\sup_{x\in S}\sqrt{k(x,x)}\leq\kappa.

Thus, they are applicable across a broad range of settings in which RKHS methods find use. This is to be contrasted with couplings based upon the Hungarian construction for the uniform empirical process.

The first, rather basic observation we make is that when the kernel is bounded, the Gaussian approximation problem (
3
) can be reduced to Gaussian approximation in
H
.

Lemma C.1
.

Let
Z
be a random element of the RKHS
H
. Then

\sup_{x\in S}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}(x)-Z(x)\right|\leq\kappa\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}-Z\right\rVert.

Proof.

Using the reproducing property, we may write

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in S}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}(x)-Z(x)\right|

\displaystyle=\sup_{x\in S}\left|\left\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}-Z,\,k_{x}\right\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left(\;\sup_{x\in S}\left\lVert k_{x}\right\rVert\right)\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}-Z\right\rVert,

where the last step is Cauchy-Schwartz.
∎

C.3
Coupling

The crucial ingredient is the following finite-dimensional coupling lemma due to Zaitsev.

Lemma C.2

(Theorem 1.1 of
Zaitsev, 1987a
)
.

Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\ldots
be an independent sequence of random variables in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
satisfying
\left\lVert\xi_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\leq a
. For each
n
there is a construction such that for each
i
,
\zeta_{i}
is a Gaussian with the same covariance as
\xi_{i}
and

\mathbb{P}\left\{\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\xi_{i}-\zeta_{i})\right\rVert_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}>\delta\right\}\leq Cm^{2}\exp\left(\frac{-c\delta\sqrt{n}}{m^{2}a}\right).

In fact, if the vectors are sub-Gaussian in the sense that for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
,

\log\{\mathbb{E}(\exp\left\langle\xi_{i},\,t\right\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{m}})\}\leq\frac{b^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}(\left\langle\xi_{i},\,t\right\rangle_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}^{2}),

then we can apply the following, slightly stronger result due to
Buzun et al., 2022
. The following is an immediate consequence of applying
(
Buzun et al., 2022
, Theorem 3)
with
L=2
and
\nu_{0}=b
.

Lemma C.3

(
Buzun et al., 2022
, Theorem 3
)
.

Set
\xi=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}
, and suppose
m\leq n
. There then exists a Gaussian random variable
\zeta\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
with covariance
\Sigma=\mathbb{E}[\xi_{i}\xi_{i}^{\top}]
such that with probability
1-\eta

\left\lVert\zeta-\xi\right\rVert_{\mathbb{R}^{m}}\lesssim\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}b^{3}\left(\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)(1/\eta)^{1/\log(mn)}.

Proof.

To lighten notation, we suppress the subscript
\mathbb{R}^{m}
for the Euclidean norm and inner product. The precise claim made was that if
X_{i}=\frac{1}{b}\Sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\xi_{i}
satisfies

\log\{\mathbb{E}(\exp\left\langle X_{i},\,t\right\rangle)\}\leq\frac{\left\lVert t\right\rVert^{2}}{2}

for all
t\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
such that
\left\lVert t\right\rVert\leq\mathtt{g}
, where
0.3\mathtt{g}\geq\sqrt{m}
, then with probability
1-e^{-t}
,

\left\lVert\xi-\zeta\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{Cb^{2}m\log(mn)^{\frac{3}{2}}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{5b^{3}m^{\frac{3}{2}}\log(mn)\log(2n)}{\sqrt{n}}\right)e^{t/\log(mn)}.

Firstly, note that under our sub-Gaussianity assumption

\displaystyle\log\{\mathbb{E}(\exp\left\langle X_{i},\,t\right\rangle)\}

\displaystyle=\log\left\{\mathbb{E}\left(\exp\left\langle\frac{1}{b}\Sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\xi_{i},\,t\right\rangle\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{b^{2}}{2}\mathbb{E}\left(\left\langle\xi_{i},\,\frac{1}{b}\Sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}t\right\rangle^{2}\right)=\frac{\left\lVert t\right\rVert^{2}}{2},

where we have used
the fact that
\Sigma^{-\frac{1}{2}}\xi_{i}
is isotropic (by construction).
Thus, the stated result indeed applies to our setting.

Since we have assumed
m\leq n
and since
\eta=e^{-t}\iff t=\log(1/\eta)
, we recover

\displaystyle\left\lVert\xi-\zeta\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\frac{Cb^{2}m\log(mn)^{\frac{3}{2}}}{\sqrt{n}}+\frac{5b^{3}m^{\frac{3}{2}}\log(mn)\log(2n)}{\sqrt{n}}\right)e^{\log(1/\eta)/\log(mn)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}b^{3}\left(\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)(1/\eta)^{1/\log(mn)},

where we use
\log(mn)\lesssim\log(n)
and
e^{\log(1/\eta)/\log(mn)}=\{e^{\log(1/\eta)}\}^{1/\log(mn)}=(1/\eta)^{1/\log(mn)}
.

∎

In our setting, we wish to construct a Gaussian element
Z
such that the distance
\Delta_{n}:=\left\|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\right)-Z\right\|

is small with high probability and such that
U_{i}
and
Z
have the same covariance structure. We will do this by projecting the
U_{i}
onto an appropriate
m
-dimensional subspace, then appealing to the
m
-dimensional coupling results.

C.4
Tail bounds

Lemma C.4

(Tail bound: Bounded)
.

Suppose that
\left\lVert U_{i}\right\rVert\leq a
almost surely. Then with probability at least
1-\eta
,

\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(I-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right\rVert\leq 2\sigma(m)\sqrt{\log(2/\eta)}\vee\frac{4a\log(2/\eta)}{\sqrt{n}}.

Proof.

We use Lemma
H.1
with
\xi_{i}=(I-\Pi_{m})U_{i}.
Then
\mathbb{E}[\xi_{i}]=\mathbb{E}\left[(I-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right]=(I-\Pi_{m})\mathbb{E}\left[U_{i}\right]=0
. Moreover,

\left\|(I-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right\|\leq\left\|U_{i}\right\|\leq a

and

\mathbb{E}\left\|(I-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right\|^{2}=\mathbb{E}\langle U_{i},(I-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\rangle=\mathbb{E}\tr\{(I-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}\}=\tr\{(I-\Pi_{m})\Sigma\}=\sigma^{2}(m).

Combining these, we can bound

\mathbb{E}\left\lVert(I-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right\rVert^{\ell}=\sigma^{2}(m)a^{\ell-2}\leq\sigma^{2}(m)a^{\ell-2}\leq\frac{\ell!}{2}\sigma^{2}(m)a^{\ell-2}

for
\ell\geq 2
.
Thus, by Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
H.2
) with
A=2a
,
B=\sqrt{\sigma^{2}(m)}
, we obtain

\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(I-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right\rVert\leq 2\left(\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}(m)\log(2/\eta)}{n}}\vee\frac{2a\log(2/\eta)}{n}\right).

Multiplying by
\sqrt{n}
completes the proof.
∎

Lemma C.5

(Tail bound: sub-Gaussian)
.

If
U_{i}
is
b
sub-Gaussian then

\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right\rVert\lesssim b\sigma(m)\sqrt{\log(2/\eta)}+\frac{b\sigma(m)\log(2/\eta)}{\sqrt{n}}.

Proof.

By Lemma
J.8
, we have

\left(\mathbb{E}\left\lVert(I-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right\rVert^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq Cb\sqrt{p}\sigma(m).

Thus we can bound

\mathbb{E}\left\lVert(I-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right\rVert^{\ell}=\ell^{\ell/2}C^{\ell}b^{\ell}\sigma^{\ell}(m)\leq\ell^{\ell/2}C^{\ell}b^{\ell}\sigma^{\ell}(m)\leq\frac{\ell!}{2}[2Cb\sigma(m)]^{\ell}

for
\ell\geq 2
, using
(1/\sqrt{2})^{\ell}\ell^{\ell/2}=(\ell/2)^{\ell/2}\leq\ell!
which follows by taking logs, and
2\leq(\sqrt{2})^{\ell}
for
\ell\geq 2
, in the last step.
Thus, applying Bernstein’s inequality (Lemma
H.2
) with
A/2=B=2Cb\sigma(m)
, we obtain that with probability
1-\eta
,

\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right\rVert\leq 2\left(\sqrt{\frac{4C^{2}b^{2}\sigma^{2}(m)\log(2/\eta)}{n}}\vee\frac{4Cb\sigma(m)\log(2/\eta)}{n}\right).

Multiplying by
\sqrt{n}
and removing universal constants completes the proof.
∎

Lemma C.6

(Tail bound: Gaussian)
.

For each
i
, let
Z_{i}
be an independent Gaussian element with covariance
\Sigma
. Then with probability at least
1-\eta
,

\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\Pi_{m})Z_{i}\right\rVert\leq\left(1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\right)\sigma(m).

Proof.

Since we are concerned with the norm of a Gaussian random element, it suffices to compute

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\Pi_{m})Z_{i}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\left\langle(1-\Pi_{m})Z_{i},\,(1-\Pi_{m})Z_{j}\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\ \mathbb{E}\left\lVert(1-\Pi_{m})Z_{i}\right\rVert^{2}=\sigma^{2}(m),

where the last step is identical to the proof in Lemma
C.4
since
Z_{i}
has covariance
\Sigma
. The result then follows from Gaussian concentration (Lemma
D.5
).
∎

C.5
Technical result

Proposition C.7

(Bounded coupling)
.

Suppose
\|U_{i}\|\leq a
almost surely. Suppose the probability space supports a countable sequence of standard Gaussian random variables
(h_{i,s})_{i,s\geq 1}
, independent of the
U_{i}
. Then, for all
m
, there exists a sequence of independent Gaussians
(Z_{i})
, each with covariance
\Sigma
, such that with probability at least
1-\eta
,

\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(U_{i}-Z_{i})\right\|\lesssim\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}\sigma(m)+\frac{am^{2}\log(m^{2}/\eta)}{\sqrt{n}}.

Proof.

Note that we may identify the range of
\Pi_{m}
, which is spanned by the top
m

eigenvectors of
\Sigma
, with
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
In particular, let
A
be the orthogonal projection of
H
onto
\mathbb{R}^{m}
defined by

A:f\mapsto(\left\langle f,\,e_{i}\right\rangle)_{1\leq i\leq m}.

Then the adjoint
A^{*}
is an isometric embedding of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
into the range of
\Pi_{m}
, and
\Pi_{m}=A^{*}A
.

Using (
C.2
), then, we can construct a coupling of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}AU_{i}
and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\zeta_{i},
where the
\zeta_{i}
are independent Gaussian vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}A^{*}AU_{i}-A^{*}\zeta_{i}\right\rVert>\delta\right)\leq Cm^{2}\exp\left(\frac{-c\delta\sqrt{n}}{am^{2}}\right).

Inverting the bound and noting that
A^{*}A=\Pi_{m}
, we find that with probability at least
1-\eta
we have

\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Pi_{m}U_{i}-A^{*}\zeta_{i}\right\rVert\leq\frac{am^{2}\log(Cm^{2}/\eta)}{c\sqrt{n}}.

(4)

Finally, we set

Z_{i}=A^{*}\zeta_{i}+\sum_{s=m+1}^{\infty}h_{i,s}\sqrt{\nu_{s}}e_{s}.

This ensures that the
Z_{i}
are independent and that
Z_{i}
and
U_{i}
both have the same covariance, namely
\Sigma
. To complete the argument, we apply the triangle inequality to see that

\begin{split}\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(U_{i}-Z_{i})\right\rVert&\leq\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Pi_{m}(U_{i}-Z_{i})\right\rVert+\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\Pi_{m})(U_{i}-Z_{i})\right\rVert\\
&\leq\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Pi_{m}(U_{i}-Z_{i})\right\rVert+\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right\rVert+\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\Pi_{m})Z_{i}\right\rVert.\end{split}

The first term is controlled by our construction above.
For the second term, by Lemma
C.4
, it holds with probability at least
1-\eta
that

\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right\rVert\leq 2\sigma(m)\sqrt{\log(2/\eta)}\vee\frac{4a\log(2/\eta)}{\sqrt{n}}.

(5)

Similarly, by Lemma
C.6
, with probability at least
1-\eta

\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\Pi_{m})Z_{i}\right\rVert\leq\left(1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\right)\sigma(m).

(6)

Applying a union bound to the events in (
4
), (
5
) and (
6
) then consolidating terms, we find that with probability at least
1-3\eta
,

\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(U_{i}-Z_{i})\right\rVert\leq\left(1+4\sqrt{\log(2/\eta)}\right)\sigma(m)+\frac{5am^{2}\log(Cm^{2}/\eta)}{\sqrt{n}}.

The result follows by replacing
\eta
with
\eta/3
and suppressing universal constants.
∎

Proposition C.8

(Sub-Gaussian coupling)
.

Suppose the
U_{i}
are
b
sub-Gaussian. Then there exists a Gaussian random variable
Z
such that for any
n\geq\log(2/\eta)
, it holds with probability
1-\eta
that

\left\lVert\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\right)-Z\right\rVert\lesssim b\sigma(m)\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}+\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}b^{3}\left(\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)(3/\eta)^{1/\log(mn)}.

Proof.

The proof is identical to the above, with the following changes. Firstly, we instead use Lemma
C.3
to couple

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}AU_{i}
and
\zeta

in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
.
This leads to the bound

\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Pi_{m}U_{i}-A^{*}\zeta_{i}\right\rVert\lesssim\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}b^{3}\left(\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)(1/\eta)^{1/\log(mn)}.

(7)

We then set

Z=A^{*}\zeta+\sum_{s=m+1}^{\infty}h_{1,s}\sqrt{\nu_{s}}\,e_{s},

and decompose

\left\lVert\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\right)-Z\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Pi_{m}U_{i}\right)-\Pi_{m}Z\right\rVert+\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right\rVert+\Bigg\|\vphantom{\sum}(1-\Pi_{m})Z\Bigg\|.

The first term we have controlled above in (
7
). For the second term, we apply Lemma
C.5
to find that

\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1-\Pi_{m})U_{i}\right\rVert\lesssim b\sigma(m)\sqrt{\log(2/\eta)}+\frac{b\sigma(m)\log(2/\eta)}{\sqrt{n}}.

The latter is dominated by the former for
n
sufficiently large:
\sqrt{\log(2/\eta)}/\sqrt{n}\leq 1\iff n\geq\log(2/\eta)
.

For the final term, we apply Lemma
C.6
(with
n=1
) to find that

\left\lVert(1-\Pi_{m})Z\right\rVert\leq\left(1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\right)\sigma(m).

Putting it all together, we have

\left\lVert\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\right)-Z\right\rVert\lesssim b\sigma(m)\sqrt{\log(2/\eta)}+\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}b^{3}\left(\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)(1/\eta)^{1/\log(mn)}.

The result follows by replacing
\eta
with
\eta/3
and suppressing universal constants.
∎

C.6
Leading cases

In Section
I
as well as the corollaries below, we specialize this bound for leading cases, including Sobolev and Gaussian RKHS.

C.6.1
Sobolev-type RKHS

In a “Sobolev-type RKHS,” the eigenvalues satisfy
\nu_{m}\leq\omega m^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>1
. In this case,
\sigma^{2}(m)\lesssim_{\beta}\omega m^{1-\beta}
. The optimal trade-off will depend on whether the summands are bounded or sub-Gaussian, as demonstrated by the following results.

Corollary C.9

(Sobolev-type RKHS: Bounded data)
.

Suppose that
\nu_{m}\leq\omega m^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>1
and
\|U_{i}\|\leq a
. Then there exists a Gaussian random variable
Z
with covariance
\Sigma
such that

\left\|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\right)-Z\right\|\lesssim_{\beta}\omega^{\frac{2}{\beta+3}}\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\right)^{\frac{\beta-1}{\beta+3}}\log\left\{\frac{6}{\eta}(\sqrt{\omega n}/a)^{4/(\beta+3)}\right\}.

Proof.

In this case
\sigma^{2}(m)\lesssim_{\beta}\omega m^{1-\beta}
by Proposition
I.2
in Section
I
.
It suffices to choose
m
correctly in Proposition
C.7
. In particular, Proposition
C.7
implies that for any
m
, we may construct a Gaussian random variable
Z
such that

\left\|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\right)-Z\right\|\lesssim_{\beta}\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}\omega^{1/2}m^{(1-\beta)/2}+\frac{am^{2}\log(m^{2}/\eta)}{\sqrt{n}}.

Solving for
m
that balances the two dominating terms,

\omega^{1/2}m^{(1-\beta)/2}=\frac{am^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\iff m=(\sqrt{\omega n}/a)^{2/(\beta+3)}.

Then

\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}\omega^{1/2}m^{(1-\beta)/2}=\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}(\sqrt{n}/a)^{(1-\beta)/(\beta+3)}\omega^{2/(\beta+3)}

and

\displaystyle\frac{am^{2}\log(m^{2}/\eta)}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}(\sqrt{\omega n}/a)^{4/(\beta+3)}\log\{(\sqrt{\omega n}/a)^{4/(\beta+3)}/\eta\}

\displaystyle=\omega^{2/(\beta+3)}(\sqrt{n}/a)^{(1-\beta)/(\beta+3)}\cdot\log\{(\sqrt{\omega n}/a)^{4/(\beta+3)}/\eta\}.

Combining constants and logarithmic factors gives us the desired rate.
∎

Corollary C.10

(Sobolev-type RKHS: Sub-Gaussian data)
.

Suppose that
\nu_{m}\leq\omega m^{-\beta}
for some
\beta>1
and
U_{i}
are
b
sub-Gaussian. Then there exists a Gaussian random variable
Z
with covariance
\Sigma
such that

\left\|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\right)-Z\right\|\lesssim_{\beta}b^{\frac{3\beta}{\beta+2}}\cdot n^{\frac{1}{2}\frac{1-\beta}{2+\beta}}\cdot\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}\frac{\beta-1}{2+\beta}}\omega^{\frac{3}{2(\beta+2)}}\cdot\log(n^{2})(3/\eta)^{\frac{1}{\log(n)}}.

Proof.

In this case
\sigma^{2}(m)\lesssim_{\beta}\omega m^{1-\beta}
by Proposition
I.2
in Section
I
.
It suffices to choose
m
correctly in Proposition
C.8
. In particular, Proposition
C.8
implies that for any
m
, we may construct a Gaussian random variable
Z
with covariance
\Sigma
such that

\left\|\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\right)-Z\right\|\lesssim_{\beta}b\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}\omega^{1/2}m^{(1-\beta)/2}+\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}b^{3}\left(\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)(3/\eta)^{1/\log(mn)}.

Solving for
m
that balances the main terms,

b\,\omega^{1/2}m^{(1-\beta)/2}=\frac{\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert^{\frac{1}{2}}_{op}b^{3}m^{3/2}}{\sqrt{n}}\iff m=(\sqrt{\omega n}b^{-2}\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{2/(\beta+2)}.

Then

\displaystyle b\omega^{1/2}m^{(1-\beta)/2}

\displaystyle=b\omega^{1/2}(\sqrt{\omega n}b^{-2}\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{(1-\beta)/(\beta+2)}

\displaystyle=b^{\frac{3\beta}{\beta+2}}\cdot n^{\frac{1}{2}\frac{1-\beta}{2+\beta}}\cdot\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1-\beta}{2+\beta}}\omega^{\frac{3}{2(\beta+2)}},

and

\displaystyle\frac{\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}b^{3}m^{3/2}}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}b^{3}(\sqrt{\omega n}b^{-2}\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{-\frac{1}{2}})^{3/(\beta+2)}}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle=b^{\frac{3\beta}{\beta+2}}\cdot n^{\frac{1}{2}\frac{1-\beta}{2+\beta}}\cdot\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{-\frac{1}{2}\frac{1-\beta}{2+\beta}}\omega^{\frac{3}{2(\beta+2)}}.

Combining constants and logarithmic factors gives us the desired rate. In particular note that

\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}
and
(3/\eta)^{1/\log(mn)}

are dominated by
(3/\eta)^{1/\log(n)}
.
∎

C.6.2
Gaussian-type RKHS

In a “Gaussian-type RKHS,”
\sigma(m)
is exponentially decaying. We obtain a
\sqrt{n}
rate of Gaussian approximation up to logarithmic factors. Notably, this includes the case where
U_{i}=k_{X_{i}}
for
k
a smooth, radial kernel on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, see
(
Belkin, 2018
;
Wendland, 2004
)
.

Corollary C.11

(Gaussian-type RKHS: Bounded data)
.

Suppose that
\nu_{m}\leq\omega\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma})
for some
\alpha,\gamma>0
and
\|U_{i}\|\leq a
almost surely. Then there exists a sequence of independent Gaussians
(Z_{i})
such that

\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(U_{i}-Z_{i})\right\|_{H}\lesssim_{\alpha,\gamma}\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{a^{2}}\right)\right]^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}\cdot\log\left\{\frac{6}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{a^{2}}\right)/\eta^{\gamma/2}\right\}.

Proof.

In this case
\sigma^{2}(m)\lesssim_{\alpha,\gamma}\omega m^{1-\gamma}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma})
by Proposition
I.2
in Section
I
. Proposition
C.7
then implies that there exists a sequence of independent Gaussians
(Z_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}
such that

\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(U_{i}-Z_{i})\right\|_{H}\lesssim_{\gamma,\alpha}\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}\left(\vphantom{\sum}\omega^{1/2}m^{(1-\gamma)/2}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)\right)+\frac{am^{2}\log(m^{2}/\eta)}{\sqrt{n}}.

Solving for
m
that balances the two dominating terms,

\omega^{1/2}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)=\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\iff m=\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{a^{2}}\right)\right]^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}.

Then

\displaystyle\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}\omega^{1/2}m^{(1-\gamma)/2}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}\omega^{1/2}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{a^{2}}\right)\right]^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\cdot\exp\left\{-\alpha\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{a^{2}}\right)\right]/2\right\}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}\omega^{1/2}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{a^{2}}\right)\right]^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\cdot\exp\left\{\left[\log\left(\frac{a}{\sqrt{\omega n}}\right)\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}\omega^{1/2}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{a^{2}}\right)\right]^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\cdot\frac{a}{\sqrt{\omega n}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{a^{2}}\right)\right]^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\cdot\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}

and

\displaystyle\frac{am^{2}\log(m^{2}/\eta)}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{a^{2}}\right)\right]^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}\log\left\{\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{a^{2}}\right)\right]^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}/\eta\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{a}{\sqrt{n}}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{a^{2}}\right)\right]^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}\cdot\frac{2}{\gamma}\log\left\{\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{a^{2}}\right)/\eta^{\gamma/2}\right\}.

Combining constants and logarithmic factors gives us the desired rate. In particular, note that

\gamma>0
implies
\frac{2}{\gamma}>\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}.

∎

Corollary C.12

(Gaussian-type RKHS: Sub-Gaussian data)
.

Suppose that
\nu_{m}\leq\omega\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma})
for some
\alpha,\gamma>0
and
U_{i}
are
b
sub-Gaussian. Then there exists a Gaussian
Z
such that

\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}-Z\right\|\lesssim\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}\frac{b^{3}}{\sqrt{n}}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}b^{4}}\right)\right]^{\frac{3}{2\gamma}}\cdot\log(n)^{2}\left(\frac{3}{\eta}\right)^{1/\log(n)}.

Proof.

In this case
\sigma^{2}(m)\lesssim_{\gamma,\alpha}\omega m^{1-\gamma}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma})
by Proposition
I.2
in Section
I
. Proposition
C.8
then implies that there exists a sequence of independent Gaussians
(Z_{i})_{1\leq i\leq m}
such that

\displaystyle\left\|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}-Z\right\|

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\gamma,\alpha}\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}b\omega^{1/2}\left\{\vphantom{\sum}m^{(1-\gamma)/2}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}b^{3}\left(\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)(3/\eta)^{1/\log(mn)}.

Solving for
m
that balances the two dominating terms,

b\omega^{1/2}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)=\frac{\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}b^{3}}{\sqrt{n}}\iff m=\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}b^{4}}\right)\right]^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}.

Then

\displaystyle\quad b\omega^{1/2}m^{(1-\gamma)/2}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)

\displaystyle=b\omega^{1/2}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}b^{4}}\right)\right]^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\cdot\exp\left\{-\alpha\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}b^{4}}\right)\right]/2\right\}

\displaystyle=b\omega^{1/2}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}b^{4}}\right)\right]^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\cdot\exp\left\{\left[\log\left(\frac{\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}b^{2}}{\sqrt{\omega n}}\right)\right]\right\}

\displaystyle=\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}b^{4}}\right)\right]^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\!\!\!\!\cdot\frac{\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{1/2}b^{3}}{\sqrt{n}}

and

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{1/2}b^{3}\left(\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)

\displaystyle=\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{1/2}\frac{b^{3}}{\sqrt{n}}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{\omega n}{\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}b^{4}}\right)\right]^{\frac{3}{2\gamma}}\log(n)^{2}.

Combining constants and logarithmic factors gives us the desired rate. In particular, note that

\gamma>0
implies
\frac{3}{2\gamma}>\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}.

The final comparison is between

\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}
and
(3/\eta)^{1/\log(mn)}
,
both of which are dominated by
(3/\eta)^{1/\log(n)}
.
∎

C.7
Supremum norm

Theorem C.13

(Sup-norm coupling)
.

With probability
1-\eta
, there exists a Gaussian process
Z
such that either:

1.

If
\|U_{i}\|\leq a
, then

\sup_{x\in S}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}(x)-Z(x)\right|\lesssim\kappa\left(\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}\sigma(m)+\frac{am^{2}\log(m^{2}/\eta)}{\sqrt{n}}\right);

2.

If instead
U_{i}
are
b
sub-Gaussian and
n\geq\log(2/\eta)
, then

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in S}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}(x)-Z(x)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\kappa\Bigg\{b\sigma(m)\sqrt{\log(6/\eta)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad+\left\lVert\Sigma\right\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}b^{3}\left(\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{n}}\right)(3/\eta)^{1/\log(mn)}\Bigg\}.

These bounds can be further specialized for Sobolev and Gaussian RKHSs using Corollaries
C.9
,
C.10
,
C.11
, and
C.12
.

Proof.

The proof is immediate from Lemma
C.1
and Propositions
C.7
and
C.8
.
∎

Appendix D
Bootstrap couplings

According to the sketch in Section
6
,
in this section we consider
Z_{\mathfrak{B}}=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}\frac{V_{i}-V_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}
and show that its conditional distribution given the data
U
is approximately Gaussian with covariance
\Sigma=\mathbb{E}(U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*})
. This is done by the following sequence of steps:

Re-writing the symmetrized bootstrap process

We show that the conditional distribution of
Z_{\mathfrak{B}}
is Gaussian with covariance
\hat{\Sigma}
, where

\hat{\Sigma}=\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}.
Note that we must use the empirically centered operator
\hat{\Sigma}
because of the symmetrized bootstrap, as detailed below.

Covariance estimation

Using concentration and approximation arguments, we prove a bound on
\lVert\hat{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\rVert
and show that this implies a bound on the distance between Gaussian distributions.

Main result

We use an extension of the Dudley-Strassen theorem due to
Monrad and Philipp, 1991
(our Lemma
D.8
) and Gaussian concentration to construct
Z^{\prime}
that is conditionally Gaussian with covariance
\Sigma
, such that
\left\lVert Z^{\prime}-Z_{\mathfrak{B}}\right\rVert
is small w.h.p. conditional upon
U
.

D.1
Notation

As before, let
(U_{1},U_{2},\ldots)
denote an i.i.d. sequence of centered random elements in
H
, and let
\Sigma=\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]
. Let
V_{i}=U_{i}+\mu
for some arbitrary (deterministic)
\mu\in H
.

Given an independent sequence of standard normal variables
(g_{1},g_{2},\ldots)
we may then consider the random series

g=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}g_{i}e_{i}
and
\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g=\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\nu_{i}}g_{i}e_{i}
, which belong almost surely to
L^{2}(U_{i})
and
H
, respectively.

Finally, we will use the notation
X\overset{U}{\sim}Y
to denote that the
\sigma(U)
-conditional distributions of the random variables
X
and
Y
are equal, i.e. for any Borel set
A
,

\mathbb{P}(X\in A|U)=\mathbb{P}(Y\in A|U)
holds

U
-almost surely.

D.2
Rewriting the symmetrized bootstrap process

To begin, we argue

\hat{\Sigma}^{1/2}g\overset{U}{\sim}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}\frac{V_{i}-V_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}.

Lemma D.1

(Covariance of symmetrized bootstrap process)
.

We have that

\hat{\Sigma}^{1/2}g\overset{U}{\sim}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}\left(\frac{V_{i}-V_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}\right).

Proof.

Since

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}\left(\frac{V_{i}-V_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)

is jointly Gaussian conditional upon
V=(V_{1},V_{2},\ldots,V_{n})
, it suffices to compute

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{h}\left[\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}\left(\frac{V_{i}-V_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right\}\otimes\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}h_{k\ell}\left(\frac{V_{k}-V_{\ell}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right\}^{*}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{h}\left[\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}\left(\frac{U_{i}-U_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right\}\otimes\left\{\frac{1}{n}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\sum_{\ell=1}^{n}h_{k\ell}\left(\frac{U_{k}-U_{\ell}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)\right\}^{*}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\{(U_{i}-U_{j})\otimes(U_{i}-U_{j})^{*}\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\{(U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*})-(U_{i}\otimes U_{j}^{*})+(U_{j}\otimes U_{j}^{*})-(U_{j}\otimes U_{i}^{*})\}.

Here we have used the fact that each summand
(i,j)
is associated with an independent standard Gaussian multiplier
h_{ij}
. By symmetry under transposition of
i
and
j
, this reduces to

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\{(U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*})-(U_{i}\otimes U_{j}^{*})\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left\{(U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*})-\left(U_{i}\otimes\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}U_{j}^{*}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*})-\left(\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\otimes\frac{1}{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}U_{j}^{*}\right)

\displaystyle=\hat{\Sigma}.

Since the conditional distribution of
\hat{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}g
is also jointly Gaussian with the same covariance, the two are equal in conditional distribution.
∎

An alternative expression will be helpful later on.

Lemma D.2

(Single sum symmetrized bootstrap process)
.

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}\left(\frac{V_{i}-V_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)=\sqrt{n}\mathbb{E}_{n}[q_{i}U_{i}],\quad q=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2n}}(h-h^{\top})\mathbf{1},\quad var(q_{i})<1.

Here,
(q_{i})
are Gaussians whose definitions are expressed in terms of
(h_{ij})
. Note that
(q_{i})
are not independent while
(h_{ij})
are.

Proof.

Write

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}\left(\frac{V_{i}-V_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}\left(\frac{U_{i}-U_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}(U_{i}h_{ij}-U_{j}h_{ij})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}U_{j}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{ij}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}\right)-\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}U_{j}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}h_{ij}\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}U_{i}q_{i}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{n}\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}q_{i}]

where

q_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2n}}\sum_{j=1}^{n}\left(h_{ij}-h_{ji}\right),\quad q=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2n}}(h-h^{\top})\mathbf{1},\quad var(q_{i})<1.

Note that the variance of
q_{i}
is strictly less than one since
h_{ij}-h_{ji}=0
when
i=j
.
∎

Corollary D.3

(Combining results)
.

In summary,

\hat{\Sigma}^{1/2}g\overset{U}{\sim}\sqrt{n}\mathbb{E}_{n}[q_{i}U_{i}],\quad q=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2n}}(h-h^{\top})\mathbf{1},\quad var(q_{i})<1.

Proof.

The result is immediate from Lemmas
D.1
and
D.2
.
∎

D.3
Covariance estimation

Next, we argue

\Sigma^{1/2}g\approx\hat{\Sigma}^{1/2}g.

As before, we will make our argument by way of finite dimensional approximation. Our approximation uses the top
m
eigenvectors, thus avoiding calculations based upon the metric entropy.

We write this subsection with some additional generality to accommodate alternative bootstraps. Throughout this subsection,
\hat{\Sigma}
denotes some feasible covariance estimator, with the property that

\hat{\Sigma}^{1/2}g\overset{U}{\sim}\sqrt{n}\mathbb{E}_{n}[q_{i}U_{i}]
for some jointly Gaussian random variables
q_{i}
that have variance at most one and that may be correlated.

The structure of the argument is as follows: (i) technical lemmas,
(ii) abstract bound (agnostic to covariance operator), and
(iii) bounding key terms (for
\hat{\Sigma}
).

D.3.1
Technical lemmas

We quote the following inequality due to Borell and independently to Sudakov, Ibragamov and Tsirelson.

Lemma D.4

(
Giné and Nickl, 2021
)
.

Let
G_{t}
be a centered Gaussian process, a.s. bounded on
T
. Then for
u>0
,

\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in T}G_{t}-\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in T}G_{t}>u\right)\vee\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{t\in T}G_{t}-\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in T}G_{t}<-u\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{-u^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}_{T}}\right)

where

\sigma^{2}_{T}:=\sup_{t\in T}\mathbb{E}G_{t}^{2}
.

The following lemma follows from Gaussian concentration.

Lemma D.5
.

Let
Z
be a Gaussian random element in a Hilbert space
H
such that
\mathbb{E}\left\lVert Z\right\rVert^{2}<\infty
. Then, with probability at least
1-\eta
,

\left\lVert Z\right\rVert\leq\left(1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\right)\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left\lVert Z\right\rVert^{2}}.

In particular, if
A:H\to H
is a trace-class operator, then with probability at least
1-\eta
w.r.t.
g
,

\lVert Ag\rVert\leq\left(1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\,\right)\lVert A\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}.

Proof.

We proceed in steps.

1.

For the first claim, we express
\lVert Z\rVert
as the supremum of a separable Gaussian process, in particular

\lVert Z\rVert=\sup_{t\in B_{H}}\left\langle t,\,Z\right\rangle=\sup_{t\in T}G_{t}.

The result follows from Gaussian concentration (Lemma
D.4
) provided we can estimate the quantities

\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in T}G_{t}=\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in B_{H}}\left\langle t,\,Z\right\rangle=\mathbb{E}\lVert Z\rVert,\quad\sigma^{2}_{T}=\sup_{t\in B_{H}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle t,\,Z\right\rangle^{2},

and show that the process is a.s. bounded.

By Jensen’s inequality we have

(\mathbb{E}\lVert Z\rVert)^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\lVert Z\rVert^{2}<\infty,

so we may deduce from Markov’s inequality that

\left\langle t,\,Z\right\rangle
is a.s. bounded.
Also by Jensen’s inequality

\sup_{t\in B_{H}}\mathbb{E}\left\langle t,\,Z\right\rangle^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in B_{H}}\left\langle t,\,Z\right\rangle^{2}=\mathbb{E}\left\lVert Z\right\rVert^{2}.

Plugging these estimates into Borell’s inequality then gives

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lVert Z\right\rVert\geq\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left\lVert Z\right\rVert^{2}}+u\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{-u^{2}}{2\mathbb{E}\left\lVert Z\right\rVert^{2}}\right).

Choosing
u=\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)\mathbb{E}\left\lVert Z\right\rVert^{2}}
and squaring gives the desired result.

2.

For the second claim, take
Z=Ag
. We need to check that
\mathbb{E}\left\lVert Ag\right\rVert^{2}=\left\lVert A\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{2}
.
Indeed, since
g=\sum_{s}g_{s}e_{s}
, we have that
Ag=\sum_{s}g_{s}Ae_{s}
and

\|Ag\|^{2}=\langle\sum_{s}g_{s}Ae_{s},\sum_{t}g_{t}Ae_{t}\rangle=\sum_{s,t}g_{s}g_{t}\langle Ae_{s},Ae_{t}\rangle.

Taking the expectation and denoting by
\iota_{s}
the
s
-th eigenvalue of
A

\mathbb{E}\|Ag\|^{2}=\sum_{s}\langle Ae_{s},Ae_{s}\rangle=\sum_{s}\langle\iota_{s}e_{s},\iota_{s}e_{s}\rangle=\sum_{s}\iota_{s}^{2}=\|A\|^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}}.

∎

Thus, the bootstrap provides a good approximation to the distribution of
\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g
whenever we can control the quantity
\lVert\hat{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}-\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}
. To accomplish this, we make use of the following finite-dimensional result.

Lemma D.6

(cf.
Wihler, 2009
)
.

Let
A
and
B
be
m\times m
real matrices. Then

\lVert A^{\frac{1}{2}}-B^{\frac{1}{2}}\rVert_{F}\leq m^{\frac{1}{4}}\lVert A-B\rVert_{F}^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

According to
Wihler, 2009
, we have

\lVert f(A)-f(B)\rVert_{F}\leq[f]_{0,\frac{1}{2}}m^{\frac{1}{4}}\lVert A-B\rVert_{F}^{\frac{1}{2}}
where
[f]_{0,\frac{1}{2}}
is the Hölder constant

[f]_{0,\frac{1}{2}}=\sup_{x\neq y}\frac{|f(x)-f(y)|}{|x-y|^{\frac{1}{2}}}.
To show that for
f(x)=\sqrt{x}
this constant is at most
1
, assume w.l.o.g. that
x>y
. In this case we have

(\sqrt{x}-\sqrt{y})^{2}=x(1-\sqrt{y/x})^{2}\leq x(1-y/x)=x-y,

since
|1-a|\leq\sqrt{|1-a^{2}|}
for
a=\sqrt{y/x}
, as the graph of the semicircle is concave over
[-1,1]
. The proof for the case
y>x
is symmetric.
∎

To prove the abstract bound, we prove some additional helpful lemmas.

Lemma D.7
.

We have

\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}=(\Pi_{m}\Sigma\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}+(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Proof.

Since

(e_{i}\otimes e_{i}^{*})(e_{j}\otimes e_{j}^{*})=\left\langle e_{i},\,e_{j}\right\rangle(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}^{*})=\delta_{ij}(e_{i}\otimes e_{j}^{*}),

it follows by definition of
\Sigma^{1/2}
and
\Pi_{m}
that

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{m}

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sqrt{\nu_{i}}e_{i}\otimes e_{i}^{*}\right)\left(\sum_{j=1}^{m}e_{j}\otimes e_{j}^{*}\right)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\sqrt{\nu_{j}}(e_{j}\otimes e_{j}^{*})

which is self-adjoint, so

\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{m}=(\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{m})^{*}=\Pi_{m}^{*}(\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}})^{*}=\Pi_{m}\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}

and
\Pi_{m}C^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{m}=\Pi_{m}^{2}C^{\frac{1}{2}}=\Pi_{m}\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}
.
Therefore

\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{m}=\Pi_{m}C^{\frac{1}{2}}=\Pi_{m}\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{m}=(\Pi_{m}\Sigma\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}},

and likewise for
\Pi_{m}^{\perp}
, replacing the indexing from
j\in[m]
to
j>m
. The result follows after noting that
\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}=\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}(\Pi_{m}+\Pi_{m}^{\perp})
.
∎

Lemma D.8

(Conditional Strassen’s Lemma;
Monrad and Philipp, 1991
)
.

Let
X
be a random variable on a probability space

(\Omega,\mathcal{S},\mathbb{P})
, and suppose that
X
takes values in a complete metric space
(S,d)
. Let
\mathcal{F}\subset\mathcal{S}
be countably generated as a
\sigma
-algebra, and assume that there exists a random variable
R
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{S},\mathbb{P})
that
is independent of
\mathcal{F}\vee\sigma(X)
. Let
G(-|\mathcal{F})
be a regular conditional distribution on the Borel sets of
(S,d)
and suppose that for some non-negative numbers
\alpha
and
\beta

\mathbb{E}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\vphantom{\sum}\mathbb{P}(X\in A|\mathcal{F})-G(\mathrm{cl}(A^{\alpha})|\mathcal{F})\right)\leq\beta

where
A^{\alpha}
is the
\alpha
-extension of
A
and the randomness in the expectation is over
\mathcal{F}
.

Then there exists a random variable
Y
with values in
S
, defined on
(\Omega,\mathcal{S},\mathbb{P})

with conditional distribution
G
satisfying

\mathbb{P}\left(\vphantom{\sum}d(X,Y)>\alpha\right)\leq\beta.

Corollary D.9
.

Under the same conditions as Lemma
D.8
, suppose there exist random variables
X^{\prime}
and
Y^{\prime}
such that (i)
X
and
X^{\prime}
have the same distribution conditional upon
\mathcal{F}
, and (ii)
\mathbb{P}(d(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})>\alpha)\leq\beta
. Then there exists some
Y
with the same conditional distribution as
Y^{\prime}
such that
\mathbb{P}(d(X,Y)>\alpha)\leq\beta
.

Proof.

According to Lemma
D.8
, it suffices to bound

\mathbb{E}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\vphantom{\sum}\mathbb{P}(X\in A|\mathcal{F})-\mathbb{P}(Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cl}(A^{\alpha})|\mathcal{F})\right)=\mathbb{E}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\vphantom{\sum}\mathbb{P}(X^{\prime}\in A|\mathcal{F})-\mathbb{P}(Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cl}(A^{\alpha})|\mathcal{F})\right),

where we have first used the fact that
X^{\prime}
and
X
are equal in conditional distribution.

Now, consider the event

E=\left\{\vphantom{\sum}d(X^{\prime},Y^{\prime})\leq\alpha\right\}.
By our assumption
\mathbb{P}(E)\geq 1-\beta
.
Also, by construction, for any Borel set
A
, we have

\left\{\vphantom{\sum}X^{\prime}\in A\right\}\cap E\subseteq\left\{\vphantom{\sum}Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cl}(A^{\alpha})\right\}.

It follows that on the event
E
,

\sup_{A\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\vphantom{\sum}X^{\prime}\in A\right\}-\mathbbm{1}\left\{\vphantom{\sum}Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cl}(A^{\alpha})\right\}\leq 0.

Moreover, since the expression inside the supremum is a difference of two probabilities, it is at most
1
everywhere. In particular, this crude bound holds on the complement of
E
. Thus, by the conditional version of Jensen’s inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\vphantom{\sum}\mathbb{P}(X^{\prime}\in A|\mathcal{F})-\mathbb{P}(Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cl}(A^{\alpha})|\mathcal{F})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{A\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbbm{1}\left\{\vphantom{\sum}X^{\prime}\in A\right\}-\mathbbm{1}\left\{\vphantom{\sum}Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cl}(A^{\alpha})\right\}\middle|\mathcal{F}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\sup_{A\in\mathcal{S}}\mathbbm{1}\left\{\vphantom{\sum}X^{\prime}\in A\right\}-\mathbbm{1}\left\{\vphantom{\sum}Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cl}(A^{\alpha})\right\}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 0\cdot\mathbb{P}(E)+1\cdot\{1-\mathbb{P}(E)\}\leq\beta.

Thus, we have verified that

\mathbb{E}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\vphantom{\sum}\mathbb{P}(X\in A|\mathcal{F})-\mathbb{P}(Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cl}(A^{\alpha})|\mathcal{F})\right)=\mathbb{E}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{S}}\left(\vphantom{\sum}\mathbb{P}(X^{\prime}\in A|\mathcal{F})-\mathbb{P}(Y^{\prime}\in\mathrm{cl}(A^{\alpha})|\mathcal{F})\right)\leq\beta,

and may conclude by applying Lemma
D.8
.
∎

Lemma D.10

(Applying operators)
.

Let
(q_{i})
be a sequence of jointly Gaussian random variables and
A:H\to H
be a self-adjoint operator. If

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}U_{i}\overset{U}{\sim}\hat{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}g

then

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}AU_{i}\overset{U}{\sim}(A\hat{\Sigma}A)^{\frac{1}{2}}g.

Proof.

Since

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}U_{i}\overset{U}{\sim}\hat{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}g,
both vectors must have covariance operator
\hat{\Sigma}
.
Now, conditional upon
U
, both random vectors in the conclusion of the Lemma are jointly Gaussian in
\vspan(U_{1},U_{2},\ldots,U_{n})\subset H
. Therefore it suffices to compute the covariance

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}AU_{i},\,u\right\rangle\left\langle\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}AU_{i},\,v\right\rangle\right]=\left\langle u,\,A\hat{\Sigma}Av\right\rangle

which follows by repeatedly using self-adjointness of
A
and the definition of the covariance.
∎

Lemma D.11
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P}\{\|(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}g\|>\alpha\}\leq\beta
. Then, there exists a random variable
G\overset{U}{\sim}\hat{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}g
such that with probability at least
1-\beta

\left\lVert(\Pi_{m}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G\right\rVert\leq\alpha.

Proof.

We verify by computing the covariance operator, which determines the law of jointly Gaussian variables, that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}U_{i}\overset{U}{\sim}\hat{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}g,\quad\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\Pi_{m}U_{i}\overset{U}{\sim}(\Pi_{m}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}g,\quad\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}U_{i}\overset{U}{\sim}(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}g.

(8)

The full computation is carried out in Lemma
D.10
.
Moreover,

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}U_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\Pi_{m}U_{i}+\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}U_{i}.

(9)

Thus we may apply Corollary
D.9
with
\mathcal{F}=\sigma(U)
, choosing the random variables to be

X=(\Pi_{m}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}g,\quad X^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\Pi_{m}U_{i},\quad Y^{\prime}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}U_{i}.

In particular, note that

\mathbb{P}\left(\vphantom{\sum}\left\lVert X^{\prime}-Y^{\prime}\right\rVert>\alpha\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lVert\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}q_{i}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}U_{i}\right\rVert>\alpha\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\vphantom{\sum}\|(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}g\|>\alpha\right)\leq\beta,

where the first equality is by equality of the random variables in (
9
), and the second is by equality in distribution (from equality in conditional distribution in (
8
)).

Thus Corollary
D.9
guarantees the existence of some random variable
Y

(
G
in the statement of this Lemma)
such that
Y
has the same conditional distribution as
Y^{\prime}
, which is the same as that of
\hat{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}g
, and

\mathbb{P}\left(\vphantom{\sum}\left\lVert X-Y\right\rVert>\alpha\right)\leq\beta,
which is precisely what was claimed.
∎

D.3.2
Abstract bound

In the abstract bound, we wish to argue that
\lVert\Sigma^{1/2}g\approx\hat{\Sigma}^{1/2}g\rVert
is small w.h.p., where the gap depends on two key quantities:

\Delta_{1}:=\|\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{HS}},\quad\Delta_{2}:=\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp}.

The bounds on these key quantities will depend on further assumptions, which we reserve for later.

Proposition D.12

(Abstract bound)
.

There exists a random variable
G\overset{U}{\sim}\hat{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}g
such that with probability at least
1-3\eta
, it holds that

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G\right\rVert\leq\left\{1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\,\right\}\left\{m^{\frac{1}{4}}\Delta_{1}^{1/2}+\Delta_{2}^{1/2}+2\sigma(m)\right\}.

Proof.

We proceed in steps.

1.

First we show that if
\mathbb{P}\{\|(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}g\|>\alpha\}\leq\beta
, then with probability at least
1-\beta
it holds that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\lVert(\Pi_{m}C\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}g-(\Pi_{m}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}g\right\rVert+\left\lVert(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}g\right\rVert+\alpha.

(10)

Let
G
be constructed as in Lemma
D.11
. By Lemma
D.7
we have

\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g=(\Pi_{m}\Sigma\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}g+(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}g.

Thus, by adding and subtracting, we have

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G

\displaystyle=(\Pi_{m}\Sigma\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}g-(\Pi_{m}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}g

\displaystyle\quad+(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}g

\displaystyle\quad+(\Pi_{m}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G.

The equation (
10
) then follows by applying the triangle inequality and noting that, by Lemma
D.11
, the final term has norm at most
\alpha
with probability at least
1-\beta
.

2.

Second, we show that if
\mathbb{P}\{\|(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}g\|>\alpha\}\leq\beta
, then with probability at least
1-\beta-2\eta
it holds that

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\{m^{\frac{1}{4}}\lVert\Sigma-\hat{\Sigma}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\sigma(m)\right\}\left\{1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\,\right\}+\alpha.

(11)

To deduce (
11
) from (
10
), apply Lemma
D.5

to each term, conditional upon the data. For the first term, we have by Lemma
D.5
that with conditional probability
1-\eta
,

\displaystyle\lVert(\Pi_{m}\Sigma\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}g-(\Pi_{m}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}g\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\lVert(\Pi_{m}\Sigma\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}-(\Pi_{m}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m})^{\frac{1}{2}}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\left(1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\,\right).

By Lemma
D.6
, this is

\displaystyle\leq m^{\frac{1}{4}}\lVert(\Pi_{m}\Sigma\Pi_{m})-(\Pi_{m}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m})\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\,\right)

\displaystyle\leq m^{\frac{1}{4}}\lVert\Pi_{m}\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert\Sigma-\hat{\Sigma}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\lVert\Pi_{m}\rVert_{op}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\,\right)

\displaystyle\leq m^{\frac{1}{4}}\lVert\Sigma-\hat{\Sigma}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\,\right)

since
\left\lVert\Pi_{m}\right\rVert_{op}=1
. For the other term we apply Lemma
D.5
similarly,
taking note that since
\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}
is self-adjoint we have

\displaystyle\lVert(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma\Pi_{m}^{\perp}}=\sqrt{\sigma^{2}(m)}.

After a union bound over events w.p.
\eta
,
\eta
, and
\beta
, the proof is complete.

3.

Finally, we show the proposition statement. By Lemma
D.5
, w.p.
1-\eta

\displaystyle\vphantom{\sum}\|(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}g\|

\displaystyle\leq\vphantom{\sum}\|(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\left(1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\,\right).

Moreover

\displaystyle\vphantom{\sum}\|(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle=\{\tr(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m}^{\perp})\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=[\tr\{\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\}+\tr(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma\Pi_{m}^{\perp})]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\leq[\tr\{\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\}]^{1/2}+\{\tr(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma\Pi_{m}^{\perp})\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle=[\tr\{\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\}]^{1/2}+\sigma(m).

In summary, w.p.
1-\eta

\vphantom{\sum}\|(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\hat{\Sigma}\Pi_{m}^{\perp})^{\frac{1}{2}}g\|\leq\left\{1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\,\right\}\left([\tr\{\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\}]^{1/2}+\sigma(m)\right)=\alpha.

Therefore by (
11
), w.p.
1-3\eta

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq\left\{1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\,\right\}\left\{m^{\frac{1}{4}}\lVert\Sigma-\hat{\Sigma}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\sigma(m)\right\}+\alpha

\displaystyle=\left\{1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\,\right\}\left\{m^{\frac{1}{4}}\lVert\Sigma-\hat{\Sigma}\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}^{\frac{1}{2}}+[\tr\{\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\}]^{1/2}+2\sigma(m)\right\}.

∎

D.3.3
Bounding key terms

The abstract bound shows with high probability,
\Sigma^{1/2}g\approx\hat{\Sigma}^{1/2}g
where the gap depends on

\Delta_{1}:=\|\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
and
\Delta_{2}:=\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp}.

We bound these key quantities under alternative assumptions. The bounds are with high probability, with respect to the randomness in
U
. Since these arguments are technical and self-contained, we present them in Appendix
J
. We summarize them in the following lemma.

Lemma D.13

(Bounding key terms for covariance estimation)
.

Under
a
-boundedness, w.p.
1-3\eta
, both of the following bounds hold:

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}

\displaystyle\leq 2\log(2/\eta)^{2}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{a^{2}\sigma^{2}(0)}{n}}\vee\frac{4a^{2}}{n}\vee\frac{8a^{2}}{n^{2}}\right\},\quad\Delta_{2}\leq 2\log(2/\eta)\left(\sqrt{\frac{a^{2}\sigma^{2}(m)}{n}}\vee\frac{2a^{2}}{n}\right).

Under
b
-sub-Gaussianity, w.p.
1-3\eta
, both of the following bounds hold:

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}

\displaystyle\leq C\log(2/\eta)^{2}\frac{b^{2}\sigma^{2}(0)}{\sqrt{n}},\quad\Delta_{2}\leq C\log(2/\eta)\frac{b^{2}\sigma^{2}(m)}{\sqrt{n}}

where
C
is a universal constant.

Proof.

See Appendix
J
.
∎

D.4
Main result

Theorem D.14
.

Suppose
a
-boundedness holds and
n\geq 2
. Then there exists a random element
G
with the same conditional distribution as the multiplier bootstrap process
\hat{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}g
such that with total probability at least
1-\eta

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G\right\rVert\leq C\log(2/\eta)^{3/2}\inf_{m\geq 1}\left\{m^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\frac{a^{2}\sigma^{2}(0)}{n}+\frac{a^{4}}{n^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}+\sigma(m)\right\}.

(12)

Suppose
b
-sub-Gaussianity holds. Then the identical statement holds with

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G\right\rVert\leq C\log(2/\eta)^{3/2}\inf_{m\geq 1}\left[m^{\frac{1}{4}}\left\{\frac{b^{4}\sigma^{4}(0)}{n}\right\}^{\frac{1}{4}}+\sigma(m)\right].

(13)

Proof.

We proceed in steps, appealing to Proposition
D.12
: w.p.
1-3\eta
,

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G\right\rVert\leq\left\{1+\sqrt{2\log(1/\eta)}\,\right\}\left\{m^{\frac{1}{4}}\Delta_{1}^{1/2}+\Delta_{2}^{1/2}+2\sigma(m)\right\}.

In particular, we substitute in different bounds from Lemma
D.13
.

1.

Proving (
12
). Since
\Delta_{1}
dominates
\Delta_{2}
, w.p.
1-6\eta
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq C\log(2/\eta)^{3/2}\left[m^{\frac{1}{4}}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{a^{2}\sigma^{2}(0)}{n}}\vee\frac{4a^{2}}{n}\vee\frac{8a^{2}}{n^{2}}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\sigma(m)\right].

If
n\geq 2
then
8a^{2}/n^{2}\leq 4a^{2}/n
. In summary,

\left\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G\right\rVert\leq C\log(2/\eta)^{3/2}\left[m^{\frac{1}{4}}\left\{\frac{a^{2}\sigma^{2}(0)}{n}+\frac{16a^{4}}{n^{2}}\right\}^{\frac{1}{4}}+\sigma(m)\right].

Optimizing over
m
and absorbing constants then yields the result.

2.

Proving (
13
). Since
\Delta_{1}
dominates
\Delta_{2}
, w.p.
1-6\eta

\displaystyle\left\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq C\log(2/\eta)^{3/2}\left[m^{\frac{1}{4}}\left\{\frac{b^{2}\sigma^{2}(0)}{\sqrt{n}}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2}}+\sigma(m)\right]

\displaystyle=C\log(2/\eta)^{3/2}\left[m^{\frac{1}{4}}\left\{\frac{b^{4}\sigma^{4}(0)}{n}\right\}^{\frac{1}{4}}+\sigma(m)\right].

∎

To simplify the exposition, we will introduce shorthand for the finite sample rate in our bootstrap couplings:

\displaystyle R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{m\geq 1}\left\{m^{\frac{1}{4}}\left(\frac{a^{2}\sigma^{2}(0)}{n}+\frac{a^{4}}{n^{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}+\sigma(m)\right\},

\displaystyle R_{\mathrm{sg}}(n)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\inf_{m\geq 1}\left\{\left(\frac{mb^{4}\sigma^{4}(0)}{n}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}+\sigma(m)\right\}.

We state the following corollary, which is useful in case we cannot sample from the multiplier bootstrap process
\hat{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}g
directly, but can sample from a proxy for it.

Corollary D.15
.

Suppose
W
and
W^{\prime}
are random variables taking values in
H
, such that (i)
\mathbb{P}(\left\lVert W^{\prime}-W\right\rVert>\delta)\leq\eta
and (ii)
W\overset{U}{\sim}\hat{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}g
. Then, under the same conditions as Theorem
D.14
, there exists a random element
Z
with the same conditional distribution as
\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g
such that with probability at least
1-\eta
, either:

\mathbb{P}\left[\vphantom{\sum}\left\lVert Z-W^{\prime}\right\rVert\geq C^{\prime}\log(6/\eta)^{3/2}R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n)+\delta\middle|U\right]\leq\eta

under
a
-boundedness with
n\geq 4a^{2}\vee 2
and
\sigma(0)\geq 1
; or

\mathbb{P}\left[\vphantom{\sum}\left\lVert Z-W^{\prime}\right\rVert\geq C^{\prime}\log(6/\eta)^{3/2}R_{\mathrm{sg}}(n)+\delta\middle|U\right]\leq\eta

under
b
-sub-Gaussianity.

Proof.

We cover the bounded case; the sub-Gaussian case is completely analogous. We proceed in steps.

1.

Let
G\overset{U}{\sim}\hat{\Sigma}^{\frac{1}{2}}g
be constructed as in Theorem
D.14
, so that

\mathbb{P}\left\{\left\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g-G\right\rVert\geq C\log(12/\eta)^{3/2}R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n)\right\}\leq\eta.

2.

Find
Z\overset{U}{\sim}\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g
such that

\mathbb{P}\{\left\lVert Z-W\right\rVert\geq C\log(12/\eta)^{3/2}R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n)\}\leq\eta.

The existence of such a
Z
follows immediately from Corollary
D.9
given the above. In particular, let
X=W
,
X^{\prime}=G
,
Y^{\prime}=\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g
, and
Y=Z
.

3.

By the triangle inequality, since we have assumed
\mathbb{P}(\left\lVert W^{\prime}-W\right\rVert\geq\delta)\leq\eta
, it holds with probability at least
1-2\eta
that

\displaystyle\left\lVert Z-W^{\prime}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert Z-W\right\rVert+\left\lVert W-W^{\prime}\right\rVert\leq C\log(12/\eta)^{3/2}R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n)+\delta.

Replacing
\eta
by
\eta/2
, the above may be rewritten

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left\lVert Z-W^{\prime}\right\rVert>C\log(24/\eta)^{3/2}R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n)+\delta\right)\leq\eta.

Thus we have

\displaystyle\eta

\displaystyle\geq\mathbb{P}\left\{\vphantom{\sum}\left\lVert Z-W^{\prime}\right\rVert\geq C\log(24/\eta)^{3/2}R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n)\right\}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{P}\left\{\vphantom{\sum}\left\lVert Z-W^{\prime}\right\rVert\geq C\log(24/\eta)^{3/2}R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n)\middle|U\right\}\right].

4.

We apply Markov’s inequality to the random variable

A(\eta)=\mathbb{P}\left\{\vphantom{\sum}\left\lVert Z-W^{\prime}\right\rVert\geq C\log(24/\eta)^{3/2}R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n)\middle|U\right\}.

Then, for any
\eta\in(0,1)
we have

\mathbb{P}_{U}\{A(\eta)>t\}\leq\mathbb{E}_{U}[A(\eta)]/t\leq\eta/t.

Choose
t=\sqrt{\eta}
. Then, the above says that w.p.
1-\sqrt{\eta}

\mathbb{P}\left\{\vphantom{\sum}\left\lVert Z-W^{\prime}\right\rVert\geq C\log(24/\eta)^{3/2}R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n)\middle|U\right\}\leq\sqrt{\eta}

so that with w.p.
1-\eta

\mathbb{P}\left\{\vphantom{\sum}\left\lVert Z-W^{\prime}\right\rVert\geq C\log(24/\eta^{2})^{3/2}R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n)\middle|U\right\}\leq\eta.

5.

Finally note that

C\{\log(24/\eta^{2})\}^{\frac{3}{2}}\leq C[\log\{(6/\eta)^{2}\}]^{\frac{3}{2}}\leq 2^{\frac{3}{2}}C\{\log(6/\eta)\}^{3/2}=C^{\prime}\{\log(6/\eta)\}^{3/2}.

∎

Appendix E
Feasible bootstrap

The sketch in Section
6
claims a high-probability bound on
\lVert\mathfrak{B}-Z_{\mathfrak{B}}\rVert
, i.e.

\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}\frac{V_{i}-V_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}-\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}h_{ij}\frac{\hat{V}_{i}-\hat{V}_{j}}{\sqrt{2}}\right\rVert.
