\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda^{3+1/\beta+2/(\beta-1)}n}\right\}^{\frac{1-\beta}{2-4\beta}}.

\left(\frac{\lambda^{\frac{6+2\beta}{\beta-1}}}{\log(n)}\wedge\lambda^{3+1/\beta+2/(\beta-1)}\right)n\gg 1.

Sobolev RKHS: Sub-Gaussian data

We proceed in steps.

1.

Once again, if
\nu_{s}\leq\omega s^{-\beta}
then by Proposition
I.2
we have
\sigma(m)\lesssim_{\beta,\omega}m^{1/2-\beta/2}
, and by Proposition
I.4
we have
\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)\lesssim_{\beta,\omega}\lambda^{-1-1/\beta}
.

2.

For
Q_{\mathrm{sg}}(n,\lambda)
, equating main terms gives

\frac{m^{1/2-\beta/2}}{\lambda}=\frac{m^{\frac{3}{2}}}{\sqrt{\lambda n}}\iff m^{-1-\beta/2}=\sqrt{\frac{\lambda}{n}}\iff m=(n/\lambda)^{1/(2+\beta)}.

Plugging this in gives

\displaystyle Q_{\mathrm{sg}}(n,\lambda)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\beta,\omega}\log^{2}(n)n^{3/(4+2\beta)-1/2}\lambda^{-3/(4+2\beta)-1/2}

\displaystyle=\log^{2}(n)n^{\frac{1-\beta}{4+2\beta}}\lambda^{\frac{-(5+\beta)}{4+2\beta}}

\displaystyle=\log^{2}(n)\left(\frac{1}{n\lambda^{\frac{5+\beta}{\beta-1}}}\right)^{\frac{\beta-1}{4+2\beta}}.

3.

For
R_{\mathrm{sg}}(n,\lambda)
, equating main terms gives

\displaystyle m^{\frac{1}{4}}\left\{\frac{\lambda^{-2-2/\beta}}{n}\right\}^{\frac{1}{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m^{1/2-\beta/2}}{\lambda}\iff\left\{\frac{\lambda^{2-2/\beta}}{n}\right\}^{\frac{1}{1-2\beta}}=m.

Using this value of
m
gives the bound

\displaystyle R_{\mathrm{sg}}(n,\lambda)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\beta,\omega}\left\{\frac{m^{1/2-\beta/2}}{\lambda}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\{\frac{\lambda^{2-2/\beta}}{n}\right\}^{\frac{1-\beta}{2-4\beta}}

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda^{2+2/\beta+2/(\beta-1)}n}\right\}^{\frac{1-\beta}{2-4\beta}}.

Gaussian RKHS: Bounded data

We proceed in steps.

1.

If
\nu_{s}\leq\omega\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma})
then by Proposition
I.2
we have
\sigma(m)\lesssim_{\,\omega,\alpha,\gamma}m^{1/2-\gamma/2}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)
, and by Proposition
I.4
we have
\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)\lesssim_{\,\omega,\alpha,\gamma}\lambda^{-1}\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}
.

2.

First we study
Q_{\mathrm{bd}}(n,\lambda)
. Plugging in our bound on
\sigma(m)
and then equating the main terms (ignoring constants and log factors) gives

\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\iff m=\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}.

\displaystyle\sigma(m)

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\omega,\alpha,\gamma}m^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2}}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\exp\left(-\alpha\frac{\log(n)}{\alpha}/2\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\exp\left(\log(n^{-1/2})\right)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}n^{-1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\omega,\alpha,\gamma}\log(n)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}n^{-1/2}.

Moreover,

\displaystyle\frac{m^{2}\log(m^{2})}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle=n^{-1/2}\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}\log\left\{\left(\frac{\log(n)}{\alpha}\right)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}\right\}\lesssim_{\omega,\gamma,\alpha}n^{-1/2}\log(n)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}\log(\log(n)).

Therefore, since
\gamma>0
implies
\frac{2}{\gamma}>\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}

Q_{\mathrm{bd}}(n,\lambda)\lesssim_{\,\omega,\alpha,\gamma}\frac{1}{\lambda\sqrt{n}}\log(n)^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}\log(\log(n)).

3.

Next we study
R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n,\lambda)
. Plugging in our bounds and equating main terms gives

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{\lambda^{-1}\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}{\lambda^{2}n}\right\}^{\frac{1}{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\frac{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}{n}\right\}^{\frac{1}{4}}

\displaystyle=\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\frac{1}{2\alpha}\log\left[\frac{n}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right]\right\}^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\quad

\displaystyle=m.

Using this value of
m
,

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma(m)}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\omega,\alpha,\gamma}\frac{1}{\lambda}m^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2}}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\{\frac{1}{2\alpha}\log\left[\frac{n}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right]\right\}^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\exp\left(-\alpha\frac{1}{2\alpha}\log\left[\frac{n}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right]/2\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\{\frac{1}{2\alpha}\log\left[\frac{n}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right]\right\}^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{4}\log\left[\frac{n}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right]\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\{\frac{1}{2\alpha}\log\left[\frac{n}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right]\right\}^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\left\{\frac{n}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right\}^{-\frac{1}{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\omega,\gamma,\alpha}\frac{1}{\lambda}\left\{\log\left[\frac{n}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right]\right\}^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\left\{\frac{n}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right\}^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

Meanwhile

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{m\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)}{n\lambda^{2}}\right\}^{\frac{1}{4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\omega,\gamma,\alpha}\left[\frac{1}{n\lambda^{2}}\left\{\frac{1}{2\alpha}\log\left[\frac{n}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right]\right\}^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\lambda^{-1}\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}\right]^{\frac{1}{4}}

\displaystyle=\lesssim_{\omega,\gamma,\alpha}\left\{\log\left[\frac{n}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right]\right\}^{\frac{1}{4\gamma}}n^{-1/4}\lambda^{-1}\{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}\}^{1/4}.

Therefore since
\gamma>0
implies
\frac{1}{2\gamma}>\frac{1}{4\gamma}
and
\frac{1}{2\gamma}>\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}
, we conclude that

R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n,\lambda)\lesssim_{\omega,\gamma,\alpha}\left\{\log\left[\frac{n}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right]\right\}^{\frac{1}{2\gamma}}\left\{\frac{n\lambda^{3}}{\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right\}^{-\frac{1}{4}}.

Gaussian RKHS: Sub-Gaussian data

We proceed in steps.

1.

If
\nu_{s}\leq\omega\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma})
then by Proposition
I.2
we have
\sigma(m)\lesssim_{\,\omega,\alpha,\gamma}m^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2}}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)
, and by Proposition
I.4
we have
\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)\lesssim_{\,\omega,\alpha,\gamma}\lambda^{-1}\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}
.

2.

For
Q_{\mathrm{sg}}(n,\lambda)
, equating main terms gives

\frac{\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)}{\lambda}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda n}}\iff m=\left\{\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{n}{\lambda}\right)\right\}^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}.

Using this value of
m
,

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma(m)}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\omega,\alpha,\gamma}\lambda^{-1}m^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2}}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{n}{\lambda}\right)\right\}^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\exp(-\alpha\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{n}{\lambda}\right)/2)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\left\{\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{n}{\lambda}\right)\right\}^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\exp\left\{\log\left(\frac{\lambda^{1/2}}{n^{1/2}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{n}{\lambda}\right)\right\}^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{1/2}n^{1/2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\omega,\alpha,\gamma}\log\left(\frac{n}{\lambda}\right)^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\frac{1}{\lambda^{1/2}n^{1/2}}.

Meanwhile

\displaystyle\frac{m^{3/2}\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{n\lambda}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{n\lambda}}\left\{\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left(\frac{n}{\lambda}\right)\right\}^{\frac{3}{2\gamma}}\lesssim_{\omega,\alpha,\gamma}\frac{\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{n\lambda}}\log\left(\frac{n}{\lambda}\right)^{\frac{3}{2\gamma}}.

Since
\gamma>0
implies
\frac{3}{2\gamma}>\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}
, w conclude that

Q_{\mathrm{sg}}(n,\lambda)\lesssim_{\,\omega,\alpha,\gamma}\frac{\log(n)^{2}}{\sqrt{n\lambda}}\log\left(\frac{n}{\lambda}\right)^{\frac{3}{2\gamma}}.

3.

Next we study
R_{\mathrm{sg}}(n,\lambda)
. Plugging in our bounds and equating main terms gives

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{\lambda^{-2}\log(1/\lambda)^{2/\gamma}}{n}\right\}^{\frac{1}{4}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\iff\left\{\frac{\lambda^{2}\log(1/\lambda)^{2/\gamma}}{n}\right\}^{\frac{1}{4}}

\displaystyle=\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)

\displaystyle\iff\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left\{\frac{n^{1/2}}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right\}\right]^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}\quad

\displaystyle=m.

Using this value of
m
,

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma(m)}{\lambda}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\omega,\gamma,\alpha}\lambda^{-1}m^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2}}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}/2)

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left\{\frac{n^{1/2}}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right\}\right]^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\exp\left[-\alpha\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left\{\frac{n^{1/2}}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right\}/2\right]

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left\{\frac{n^{1/2}}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right\}\right]^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\exp\left[\log\left\{\frac{\lambda^{1/2}\log(1/\lambda)^{1/2\gamma}}{n^{1/4}}\right\}\right]

\displaystyle=\lambda^{-1}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left\{\frac{n^{1/2}}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right\}\right]^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\frac{\lambda^{1/2}\log(1/\lambda)^{1/2\gamma}}{n^{1/4}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\omega,\alpha,\gamma}\log\left\{\frac{n^{1/2}}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right\}^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}}\frac{\log(1/\lambda)^{1/2\gamma}}{\lambda^{1/2}n^{1/4}}.

Meanwhile

\displaystyle\left\{\frac{m\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)^{2}}{n}\right\}^{1/4}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\omega,\gamma,\alpha}\left[\frac{1}{\alpha}\log\left\{\frac{n^{1/2}}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right\}\right]^{\frac{1}{4\gamma}}n^{-1/4}\left\{\lambda^{-1}\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}\right\}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\omega,\gamma,\alpha}\log\left\{\frac{n^{1/2}}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right\}^{\frac{1}{4\gamma}}n^{-1/4}\left\{\lambda^{-1}\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}\right\}^{1/2}.

Since
\gamma>0
implies
\frac{1}{2\gamma}>\frac{1}{4\gamma}
and
\frac{1}{2\gamma}>\frac{1-\gamma}{2\gamma}
, we conclude that

R_{\mathrm{bd}}(n,\lambda)\lesssim_{\,\omega,\alpha,\gamma}\log\left\{\frac{n^{1/2}}{\lambda\log(1/\lambda)^{1/\gamma}}\right\}^{\frac{1}{2\gamma}}\frac{\log(1/\lambda)^{1/2\gamma}}{\lambda^{1/2}n^{1/4}}.

Summary

Spectrum

Poly. :
\nu_{s}\leq\omega s^{-\beta}

Exp. :
\nu_{s}\leq\omega\exp(-\alpha s^{\gamma})

Data

Bounded

sub-Gaussian

Bounded

sub-Gaussian

Q_{\bullet}

\lambda^{-1}n^{\frac{1-\beta}{6+2\beta}}

\lambda^{\frac{-(5+\beta)}{4+2\beta}}n^{\frac{1-\beta}{4+2\beta}}

\lambda^{-1}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}

(\lambda n)^{-\frac{1}{2}}

R_{\bullet}

\left({\lambda^{3+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{2}{\beta-1}}n}\right)^{\frac{1-\beta}{4\beta-2}}

\left({\lambda^{2+\frac{2}{\beta}+\frac{2}{\beta-1}}n}\right)^{\frac{1-\beta}{4\beta-2}}

\lambda^{-3/4}n^{-1/4}

\lambda^{-1/2}n^{-1/4}

Table 11
:
Summary of results under different assumptions. The initial two rows present rates (suppressing log factors) for the quantities
Q_{\mathrm{bd}}
,
Q_{\mathrm{sg}}
,
R_{\mathrm{bd}}
, and
R_{\mathrm{sg}}
.

These values correspond to
(Q,R)
in Table
7
. We now derive the remaining entries in that table. We proceed in steps.

1.

L
. Lemma
G.13
implies
L\asymp\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)^{1/2}-\lambda^{-1/2}
. Note that
\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)=\sigma^{2}\{(T+\lambda)^{-2}T,0\}=\psi(0,2)
, so we appeal to Proposition
I.5
.
For polynomial decay, we show
\psi(0,2)\gtrsim\lambda^{-1-1/\beta}
. Hence, for
\beta>1
,

L\asymp\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)^{1/2}-\lambda^{-1/2}\gtrsim\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2\beta}}-\lambda^{-1/2}=\lambda^{-1/2}(\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2\beta}}-1)\gtrsim\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}-\frac{1}{2\beta}}.

For exponential decay, we show
\psi(0,2)\gtrsim\lambda^{-1}
, ignoring log factors.
Hence

L\asymp\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)^{1/2}-\lambda^{-1/2}\gtrsim\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}-\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}\asymp\lambda^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

2.

B\ll L
. For polynomial decay

n\lambda^{r-1}\ll\lambda^{-1-1/\beta}\iff\lambda^{r+1/\beta}\ll n^{-1}.

For exponential decay

n\lambda^{r-1}\ll\lambda^{-1}\iff\lambda^{r}\ll n^{-1}.

3.

Q+R\ll L
.

(a)

Polynomial decay, bounded data. We have

\displaystyle Q\ll L\iff\lambda^{-2}n^{\frac{1-\beta}{3+\beta}}\ll\lambda^{-1-1/\beta}\iff n^{\frac{1-\beta}{3+\beta}}\ll\lambda^{1-1/\beta}

\displaystyle\iff n^{\frac{1-\beta}{3+\beta}}\ll\lambda^{(\beta-1)/\beta}\iff n^{\frac{1}{3+\beta}}\gg\lambda^{-1/\beta}\iff n^{\frac{-\beta}{3+\beta}}\ll\lambda.

Then

\displaystyle R\ll L\iff\{\lambda^{3+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{2}{\beta-1}}n\}^{\frac{1-\beta}{2\beta-1}}

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{-1-1/\beta}

\displaystyle\iff\{\lambda^{3+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{2}{\beta-1}}n\}^{\frac{1-\beta}{2\beta-1}}

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{-(\beta+1)/\beta}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda^{3+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{2}{\beta-1}}n

\displaystyle\gg\lambda^{-\frac{(\beta+1)(2\beta-1)}{\beta(1-\beta)}}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda^{3+\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{2}{\beta-1}}n

\displaystyle\gg\lambda^{\frac{(\beta+1)(2\beta-1)}{\beta(\beta-1)}}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda^{\frac{3\beta(\beta-1)+(\beta-1)+2\beta}{\beta(\beta-1)}}n

\displaystyle\gg\lambda^{\frac{(\beta+1)(2\beta-1)}{\beta(\beta-1)}}

\displaystyle\iff n

\displaystyle\gg\lambda^{\frac{(\beta+1)(2\beta-1)-[3\beta(\beta-1)+(\beta-1)+2\beta]}{\beta(\beta-1)}}

To simplify the numerator, note that

\displaystyle(\beta+1)(2\beta-1)-[3\beta(\beta-1)+(\beta-1)+2\beta]

\displaystyle=2\beta^{2}+\beta-1-[3\beta^{2}-3\beta+\beta-1+2\beta]

\displaystyle=-\beta^{2}+\beta=\beta(1-\beta).

Therefore the above simplifies to
n\gg\lambda^{-1}\iff\lambda\gg 1/n
.

Since
1>\beta/(3+\beta)
, the
Q
condition binds.
Combining
Q+R\ll L
with
B\ll L
,

\displaystyle n^{\frac{-\beta}{3+\beta}}\ll\lambda\ll n^{-\beta/(r\beta+1)}\iff 3+\beta\leq r\beta+1\iff r\geq 1+2/\beta.

(b)

Polynomial decay, sub-Gaussian data. We have

\displaystyle Q\ll L\iff\lambda^{-\frac{5+\beta}{2+\beta}}n^{\frac{1-\beta}{2+\beta}}

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{-1-1/\beta}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2+\beta}}n^{\frac{1-\beta}{2+\beta}}

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{-1/\beta}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda^{-3}n^{1-\beta}

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{-(2+\beta)/\beta}

\displaystyle\iff n^{-1(\beta-1)}

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{(2\beta-2)/\beta}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda

\displaystyle\gg n^{-\beta/2}.

Then

\displaystyle R\ll L\iff\{\lambda^{2+\frac{2}{\beta}+\frac{2}{\beta-1}}n\}^{\frac{1-\beta}{2\beta-1}}

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{-1-1/\beta}

\displaystyle\iff\{\lambda^{2+\frac{2}{\beta}+\frac{2}{\beta-1}}n\}^{\frac{1-\beta}{2\beta-1}}

\displaystyle\ll\lambda^{-(\beta+1)/\beta}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda^{2+\frac{2}{\beta}+\frac{2}{\beta-1}}n

\displaystyle\gg\lambda^{-\frac{(\beta+1)(2\beta-1)}{\beta(1-\beta)}}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda^{2+\frac{2}{\beta}+\frac{2}{\beta-1}}n

\displaystyle\gg\lambda^{\frac{(\beta+1)(2\beta-1)}{\beta(\beta-1)}}

\displaystyle\iff\lambda^{\frac{2\beta(\beta-1)+2(\beta-1)+2\beta}{\beta(\beta-1)}}n

\displaystyle\gg\lambda^{\frac{(\beta+1)(2\beta-1)}{\beta(\beta-1)}}

\displaystyle\iff n

\displaystyle\gg\lambda^{\frac{(\beta+1)(2\beta-1)-[2\beta(\beta-1)+2(\beta-1)+2\beta]}{\beta(\beta-1)}}

To simplify the numerator, note that

\displaystyle(\beta+1)(2\beta-1)-[2\beta(\beta-1)+2(\beta-1)+2\beta]

\displaystyle=2\beta^{2}+\beta-1-[2\beta^{2}-2\beta+2\beta-2+2\beta]

\displaystyle=1-\beta.

Therefore the above simplifies to
n\gg\lambda^{-1/\beta}\iff\lambda\gg n^{-\beta}
.

Since
\beta>\beta/2
, the
Q
condition binds.
Combining
Q+R\ll L
with
B\ll L
,

\displaystyle n^{\frac{-\beta}{2}}\ll\lambda\ll n^{-\beta/(r\beta+1)}\iff\frac{1}{2}\geq\frac{1}{r\beta+1}\iff r\geq 1/\beta.

(c)

Exponential decay, bounded data. The two conditions are equivalent:

Q\ll L\iff\lambda^{-2}n^{-1}\ll\lambda^{-1}\iff\lambda\gg n^{-1};

R\ll L\iff\lambda^{-\frac{3}{2}}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ll\lambda^{-1}\iff\lambda^{1/2}\gg n^{-1/2}.

Combining
Q+R\ll L
with
B\ll L
,

\displaystyle n^{-1}\ll\lambda\ll n^{-1/r}\iff 1\geq\frac{1}{r}\iff r\geq 1.

(d)

Exponential decay, sub-Gaussian data. The two conditions are equivalent:

Q\ll L\iff\lambda^{-1}n^{-1}\ll\lambda^{-1}\iff 1\gg n^{-1};

R\ll L\iff\lambda^{-1}n^{-\frac{1}{2}}\ll\lambda^{-1}\iff 1\gg n^{-1/2}.

Combining
Q+R\ll L
with
B\ll L
,

0\ll\lambda\ll n^{-1/r}.

Appendix H
Concentration inequalities for Bahadur representation (Sections
B
and
E
)

H.1
Concentration

We quote a version of Bernstein’s inequality for random variables in a Hilbert space.

Lemma H.1

(Theorem 3.3.4 of
Yurinsky, 1995
)
.

Let
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\ldots
be an independent sequence of random variables in a Hilbert space that satisfy
\mathbb{E}[\xi_{i}]=0
. If, for some
B,A>0
and all
\ell\geq 2
it holds that

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}\|\xi_{i}\|^{\ell}\leq\frac{\ell!}{2}B^{2}A^{\ell-2},

then for
\delta>0

\mathbb{P}\left(\max_{m\in[n]}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{m}\xi_{i}\right\|\geq\delta B\right)\leq 2\exp\left\{\frac{-\delta^{2}/2}{1+\delta A/B}\right\}.

A useful corollary, frequently used in the kernel ridge regression literature
(
Caponnetto and De Vito, 2007
)
, is as follows.

Lemma H.2
.

Suppose that
\xi_{i}
are i.i.d. random elements of a Hilbert space, which satisfy, for all
\ell\geq 2

\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\xi_{i}-\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}\right\rVert^{\ell}\leq\frac{1}{2}\ell!B^{2}(A/2)^{\ell-2}.

Then
for any
0<\eta<1
it holds with probability at least
1-\eta
that

\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\xi_{i}-\mathbb{E}[\xi_{i}]\right\rVert\leq 2\left(\sqrt{\frac{B^{2}\log(2/\eta)}{n}}\vee\frac{A\log(2/\eta)}{n}\right)\leq 2\log(2/\eta)\left\{\frac{A}{n}\vee\sqrt{\frac{B^{2}}{n}}\right\}.

In particular, this holds if

\mathbb{E}(\left\lVert\xi_{i}\right\rVert^{2})\leq B^{2}
and
\left\lVert\xi_{i}\right\rVert\leq A/2
almost surely.

Remark H.3
.

Note that the result of Lemma
H.2
for bounded random vectors may be recovered from Talagrand’s concentration inequality for empirical processes (see
Massart, 2000
), by considering the special case where the sample paths are linear and the parameter space is an ellipsoid.

H.2
Bounds for sums

Lemma H.4
.

With probability
1-\eta
,

\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}T_{\lambda}^{-1}\varepsilon_{i}k_{X_{i}}\right\rVert\leq 2\bar{\sigma}\ln(2/\eta)\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)}{n}}\vee\frac{2\kappa}{n\lambda}\right\}.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbb{E}[T_{\lambda}^{-1}\varepsilon_{i}k_{X_{i}}]=\mathbb{E}[T_{\lambda}^{-1}k_{X_{i}}\mathbb{E}(\varepsilon_{i}|X_{i})]=0
. It therefore suffices to show
\left\lVert T_{\lambda}^{-1}\varepsilon_{i}k_{X_{i}}\right\rVert\leq\left\lVert T_{\lambda}^{-1}\right\rVert_{op}\left\lVert\varepsilon_{i}k_{X_{i}}\right\rVert\leq\frac{\kappa\bar{\sigma}}{\lambda}
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\lVert T_{\lambda}^{-1}\varepsilon_{i}k_{X_{i}}\right\rVert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\sigma}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert T_{\lambda}^{-1}k_{X_{i}}\right\rVert^{2}=\bar{\sigma}^{2}\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{\mathbb{E}\left\langle k_{X_{i}},\,e_{s}\right\rangle^{2}}{(\nu_{s}+\lambda)^{2}}=\bar{\sigma}^{2}\sum_{s=1}^{\infty}\frac{\nu_{s}}{(\nu_{s}+\lambda)^{2}}=\bar{\sigma}^{2}\mathfrak{n}(\lambda).

Plugging these estimates into Lemma
H.2
gives the result.
∎

Lemma H.5
.

With probability
1-\eta
,

\left\lVert\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}T_{\lambda}^{-1}(T_{i}-T)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq 2\kappa\ln(2/\eta)\left\{\sqrt{\frac{\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)}{n}}\vee\frac{2\kappa}{n\lambda}\right\}.

Proof.

Note that
\mathbb{E}[T_{\lambda}^{-1}(T_{i}-T)]=T_{\lambda}^{-1}\mathbb{E}[T_{i}-T]=0
. Also,

\left\lVert T_{\lambda}^{-1}(T_{i}-T)\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\left\lVert T_{\lambda}^{-1}\right\rVert_{op}\left\lVert T_{i}-T\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\frac{2\kappa^{2}}{\lambda}.

Finally, using the fact that
T
,
T_{i}
and
T_{\lambda}^{-1}
are self-adjoint,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\lVert T_{\lambda}^{-1}(T_{i}-T)\right\rVert^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\tr[T_{\lambda}^{-1}(T_{i}-T)^{2}T_{\lambda}^{-1}]

\displaystyle=\tr\mathbb{E}[T_{\lambda}^{-1}(T_{i}-T)^{2}T_{\lambda}^{-1}]

\displaystyle=\tr\{\mathbb{E}[T_{\lambda}^{-1}T_{i}^{2}T_{\lambda}^{-1}]-T_{\lambda}^{-1}TT_{\lambda}^{-1}\}.

Since both operators are positive definite, this is

\displaystyle\leq\tr\{\mathbb{E}[T_{\lambda}^{-1}T_{i}^{2}T_{\lambda}^{-1}].

Since
T_{i}=k_{X_{i}}\otimes k_{X_{i}}^{*}
,
\|T_{i}\|_{op}\leq\|T_{i}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}=\left\lVert k_{X_{i}}\right\rVert^{2}\leq\kappa^{2}
, and hence

\displaystyle\leq\kappa^{2}\mathbb{E}[|\tr\{T_{\lambda}^{-1}T_{i}T_{\lambda}^{-1}\}|].

Since the trace is almost surely positive, this is

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}\mathbb{E}[\tr\{T_{\lambda}^{-1}T_{i}T_{\lambda}^{-1}\}]

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}\tr[T_{\lambda}^{-1}TT_{\lambda}^{-1}].

Finally, since
T
commutes with
T_{\lambda}^{-1}
by construction, this is

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}\tr[T_{\lambda}^{-2}T]=\kappa^{2}\mathfrak{n}(\lambda).

Plugging these estimates into Lemma
H.2
gives the result.
∎

Appendix I
Spectral bounds for Gaussian couplings (Section
C
)

We characterize the behavior of

\sigma^{2}(m)=\sum_{s>m}\nu_{s}
and
\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)=\tr(T_{\lambda}^{-2}T)

under various regimes for the spectrum of
\Sigma
. Such regimes can be deduced directly from regularity of the kernel function
k

(
Belkin, 2018
)
.

I.1
Spectral decay

We need the following technical lemma on the size of the incomplete Gamma function.

Lemma I.1

(
Natalini and Palumbo, 2000
)
.

Let the (upper) incomplete gamma function
\Gamma
be given by
\Gamma(z,x)\coloneqq\int_{x}^{\infty}u^{z-1}e^{-u}\,du.

For
z>1
and
x\geq 0
we have

x^{z-1}e^{-x}<\Gamma(z,x)\leq\frac{x^{z}e^{-x}}{x-z}.

Proposition I.2
.

Suppose
\nu_{s}\leq\bar{\nu}(s):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
for some non-increasing positive function
\bar{\nu}(s)
. Then, it holds that

\sigma^{2}(m)=\sum_{s=m+1}^{\infty}\nu_{s}\leq\int_{m}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(s)\,ds.

In particular, if
\nu_{s}\leq\omega s^{-\beta}
for
\beta>1
, then we have

\sigma^{2}(m)\leq\frac{\omega m^{1-\beta}}{\beta-1}.

Similarly, if
\nu_{s}\leq\omega\exp(-\alpha s^{\gamma})
for
\alpha,\omega,\gamma>0
, then we have

\sigma^{2}(m)\leq C(\gamma,\alpha)\omega m^{1-\gamma}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma}),

for a constant
C(\gamma,\alpha)
which depends only on
\alpha
and
\gamma
(and not on
m
).

Proof.

We upper bound

\sigma^{2}(m)=\sum_{s=m+1}^{\infty}\nu_{s}\leq\int_{m}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(s)\,ds,
where
\bar{\nu}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
is any non-increasing function with
\bar{\nu}(s)\geq\nu_{s}
. The first bound follows from taking
\bar{\nu}(s)=\omega s^{-\beta}
and computing the resulting integral exactly. For the second bound, we take
\bar{\nu}(s)=\omega\exp(-\alpha s^{\gamma})
and obtain

\frac{\sigma^{2}(m)}{\omega}\leq\int_{m}^{\infty}\exp(-\alpha s^{\gamma})\,ds.

To bound the right hand side, we proceed in steps.

1.

Making the substitution
u=\alpha s^{\gamma}
gives us

\int_{m}^{\infty}\exp(-\alpha s^{\gamma})\,ds=\gamma^{-1}\alpha^{\frac{-1}{\gamma}}\int_{\alpha m^{\gamma}}^{\infty}\exp(-u)u^{\nicefrac{{(1-\gamma)}}{{\gamma}}}\,du.

2.

We have

\int_{\alpha m^{\gamma}}^{\infty}\exp(-u)u^{\nicefrac{{(1-\gamma)}}{{\gamma}}}\,du=\Gamma(1/\gamma,\alpha m^{\gamma})
where
\Gamma(z,x)
is the incomplete Gamma function (cf. Lemma
I.1
). By Lemma
I.1
,

\Gamma(z,x)\leq\frac{x^{z}e^{-x}}{x-z}
, so

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma^{2}(m)}{\omega}\leq\gamma^{-1}\alpha^{\frac{-1}{\gamma}}\Gamma\Big(1/\gamma,\alpha m^{\gamma}\Big)\leq\gamma^{-1}\alpha^{\frac{-1}{\gamma}}\frac{(\alpha m^{\gamma})^{1/\gamma}e^{-\alpha m^{\gamma}}}{\alpha m^{\gamma}-1/\gamma}=\frac{m\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma})}{\alpha\gamma m^{\gamma}-1}.

3.

Finally we absorb constants.
When
m\geq\{2/(\alpha\gamma)\}^{1/\gamma}
, we have that
\alpha\gamma m^{\gamma}\geq 2
and hence
\alpha\gamma m^{\gamma}-1\geq\alpha\gamma m^{\gamma}/2
. Thus, the final expression is at most
(2/\alpha\gamma)m^{1-\gamma}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma})
.
For
m<\{2/(\alpha\gamma)\}^{1/\gamma}
, we decompose the sum as

\sum_{s=m+1}^{\lfloor\{2/(\alpha\gamma)\}^{1/\gamma}\rfloor}\nu_{s}+\sum_{s=\lceil\{2/(\alpha\gamma)\}^{1/\gamma}\rceil}^{\infty}\nu_{s}.

The latter sum is bounded by the previous case. Focusing on the former sum,

\sum_{s=m+1}^{\{2/(\alpha\gamma)\}^{1/\gamma}}\nu_{s}\leq\sum_{s=1}^{\{2/(\alpha\gamma)\}^{1/\gamma}}\nu_{s}\leq\{2/(\alpha\gamma)\}^{1/\gamma}\nu_{1}\leq\{2/(\alpha\gamma)\}^{1/\gamma}\cdot\omega\exp(-\alpha)

since

\nu_{1}\leq\bar{\nu}(1)\leq\omega\exp(-\alpha 1^{\gamma})=\omega\exp(-\alpha).

These terms may be absorbed into a constant depending only on
\omega,\alpha,\gamma
, and which is linear in
\omega
.

∎

I.2
Complexity measures

Recall the definition of the ellipse
\mathfrak{E}=\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}B
, where
B
is the unit ball in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P})
. We use the results of
Wei et al., 2020
to briefly sketch the claim that, under suitable regularity conditions, the local width
\sigma(\Sigma,m)
is roughly comparable to the local Gaussian complexity of
\mathfrak{E}
at scale
\delta=\mathrm{ent}_{m}(\mathfrak{E})
, which we denote by
\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{E}\cap\delta B)
.

First, some technicalities. For
S_{m}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, the Gaussian width and Gaussian complexity are given by

\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in S_{m}}\left\langle g,\,s\right\rangle
and
\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in S_{m}}|\left\langle g,\,s\right\rangle|,
respectively, where
g
is an isotropic Gaussian vector. These quantities immediately generalize to compact subsets of a separable Hilbert space by finite-dimensional approximation. Moreover, when
S_{m}
contains the origin (as in our setting) they are equivalent up to a constant
(
Vershynin, 2018
, Chapter 7)
. Thus, as is standard, we can use the results of
Wei et al., 2020
, who study the Gaussian width of finite-dimensional sets, to characterize the Gaussian complexity in our setting.

Lemma I.3
.

Suppose either

1.

\nu_{s}(\Sigma)\asymp s^{-\beta}
for
\beta>1
, or

2.

\nu_{s}(\Sigma)\asymp\exp(-\alpha s^{\gamma})
for
\alpha>0
and
\gamma\in(0,1)
.

Then, for sufficiently small
\delta=\mathrm{ent}_{m}(\mathfrak{E})
, we have
\sigma(\Sigma,m)\lesssim\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{E}\cap\delta B)
.

Proof.

Both cases were covered by
Wei et al., 2020
, and the subsequent discussion in Section 4 of that paper.

In particular, it was shown that given a scale parameter
\delta
, one can compute the “critical dimension”
m^{*}
as the smallest
m
such that
\sqrt{\nu_{m}(\Sigma)}\leq 9\delta/10
. Then, according to regularity conditions which were checked in the aforementioned Example 4, one has by
Wei et al., 2020
that for small enough
\delta
,

\delta\sqrt{m^{*}}\lesssim\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{E}\cap\delta B).

It then follows from our bounds in Proposition
I.2
above that

\delta\sqrt{m^{*}}\gtrsim\sqrt{m^{*}\nu_{m^{*}}(\Sigma)}\gtrsim\sigma(\Sigma,m^{*})

in both of the above examples.

Next,
Wei et al., 2020
implies (after inverting the entropy function) that in the same setting

\delta\lesssim\mathrm{ent}_{m^{*}}(\mathfrak{E}\cap\delta B)\leq\mathrm{ent}_{m^{*}}(\mathfrak{E})=\delta^{\prime}.

Our sketch is then complete by monotonicity of
\mathcal{G}
since

\sigma(\Sigma,m^{*})\lesssim\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{E}\cap\delta B)\leq\mathcal{G}(\mathfrak{E}\cap\delta^{\prime}B).

∎

I.3
Effective dimension

In this subsection, we will derive bounds on the quantities

\psi(m,c)=\sum_{s=m+1}^{\infty}\frac{\nu_{s}}{(\nu_{s}+\lambda)^{c}},

where
c\geq 1
, which are crucial for our analysis. Such quantities arise frequently in studies of ridge regression; see, for example, the “effective dimension” appearing in
Caponnetto and De Vito, 2007
. In particular note that according to our definition,
\mathfrak{n}(\lambda)=\psi(0,2)
. We provide matching upper and lower bounds.

Proposition I.4

(Upper bound on effective dimension)
.

Suppose
\nu_{s}\leq\bar{\nu}(s):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
for some non-increasing positive function
\bar{\nu}(s)
, and let
c\geq 1
be given. Then

\psi(m,c)\leq\lambda^{1-c}\inf_{s\geq m}\left\{(s-m+1)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{s}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt\right\}.

In particular, if
\nu_{s}\leq\omega s^{-\beta}
for some
\omega>0
and
\beta>1
, and if
\lambda\leq\omega
,

\psi(m,c)\leq\frac{\omega(2\beta-1)}{\beta-1}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{c+1/\beta-1}}\wedge\frac{m^{1-\beta}}{\lambda^{c}}\right).

If
\nu_{s}\leq\omega e^{-\alpha s^{\gamma}}
for some
\alpha,\omega,\gamma>0
, and if
\lambda\leq\omega/e^{\alpha}
, then

\psi(m,c)\leq C(\alpha,\omega,\gamma)\left\{\frac{1}{\lambda^{c-1}}\left(\frac{\log(\omega/\lambda)}{\alpha}\right)^{1/\gamma}\wedge\frac{m^{1-\gamma}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma})}{\lambda^{c}}\right\}

for some constant
C(\alpha,\omega,\gamma)
which depends only on
\alpha
,
\omega
, and
\gamma
.

Proof of Proposition
I.4
.

We proceed in steps.

1.

First we note

\frac{\nu_{s}}{(\nu_{s}+\lambda)^{c}}\leq\frac{\nu_{s}}{\lambda^{c}},\quad\frac{\nu_{s}}{(\nu_{s}+\lambda)^{c}}\leq\lambda^{1-c}.

The former holds since
\nu_{s}\geq 0
.
The latter follows from maximizing the function
f(u)=\frac{u}{(u+\lambda)^{c}}
at
u=\lambda/(c-1)
.

2.

Combining the two, we have for any
k\geq m
that

\displaystyle\psi(m,c)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s=m+1}^{\infty}\frac{\nu_{s}}{(\nu_{s}+\lambda)^{c}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{s=m+1}^{k}\lambda^{1-c}+\sum_{s=k+1}^{\infty}\frac{\nu_{s}}{\lambda^{c}}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{1-c}\left\{(k-m)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{k}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt\right\}.

Since
k
was arbitrary, we can further deduce that

\displaystyle\psi(m,c)

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{1-c}\inf_{k\geq m}\left\{(k-m)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{k}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt\right\}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{1-c}\inf_{s\geq m}\left\{(\lceil s\rceil-m)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\lceil s\rceil}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{1-c}\inf_{s\geq m}\left\{(\lceil s\rceil-m)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{s}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt\right\},

where the latter infimum is over real numbers
s
.

3.

Optimizing the bounds. In the infimum, consider
s=m
. Then

\psi(m,c)\leq\lambda^{-c}\int_{m}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt.

Alternatively, consider the choice
s^{*}=\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)\vee m
. Then

\psi(m,c)\leq\lambda^{1-c}\left\{1+s^{*}-m+\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{s^{*}}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt\right\}.

In particular, choosing
m=0
and
s^{*}=\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)
, we see

\psi(m,c)\leq\psi(0,c)\leq\lambda^{1-c}\left\{1+\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt\right\}.

Combining both bounds,

\psi(m,c)\leq\lambda^{1-c}\left\{1+\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt\right\}\wedge\frac{1}{\lambda^{c}}\int_{m}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt.

4.

Polynomial decay. Now, if
\bar{\nu}(s)=\omega s^{-\beta}
then we have
\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)=(\omega/\lambda)^{1/\beta}
, and as argued in Proposition
I.2
,

\int_{s}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt=\frac{\omega s^{1-\beta}}{\beta-1}.

Plugging this into the first term in the optimized bound,

\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt=\frac{1}{\lambda}\frac{\omega\{(\omega/\lambda)^{1/\beta}\}^{1-\beta}}{\beta-1}=\lambda^{-1-(1-\beta)/\beta}\omega^{1+(1-\beta)/\beta}\frac{1}{\beta-1}=\lambda^{-1/\beta}\cdot\omega^{1/\beta}\cdot(\beta-1)^{-1}

so that, when
\omega\geq\lambda
,

\displaystyle\lambda^{1-c}\left\{1+\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt\right\}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{1-c}\left\{1+(\omega/\lambda)^{1/\beta}+\lambda^{-1/\beta}\cdot\omega^{1/\beta}\cdot(\beta-1)^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{1-c}\left\{2(\omega/\lambda)^{1/\beta}+\lambda^{-1/\beta}\cdot\omega^{1/\beta}\cdot(\beta-1)^{-1}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left(2\omega^{1/\beta}+\frac{\omega^{1/\beta}}{{\beta-1}}\right)\lambda^{1-c-1/\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{2\beta-1}{\beta-1}\omega^{1/\beta}\lambda^{1-c-1/\beta}.

Therefore the overall bound is

\frac{2\beta-1}{\beta-1}\omega^{1/\beta}\lambda^{1-c-1/\beta}\wedge\frac{\omega m^{1-\beta}}{\lambda^{c}(\beta-1)}\leq\frac{\omega(2\beta-1)}{\beta-1}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{c+1/\beta-1}}\wedge\frac{m^{1-\beta}}{\lambda^{c}}\right).

5.

Exponential decay. If
\bar{\nu}(t)\leq\omega\exp(-\alpha t^{\gamma})
then we have
\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)=\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{1/\gamma}
and, as argued in Proposition
I.2
,

\int_{s}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt\leq C\omega s^{1-\gamma}\exp(-\alpha s^{\gamma}).

Plugging this into the first term in the optimized bound,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}C\omega[\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{1/\gamma}]^{1-\gamma}\cdot\exp(-\alpha[\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{1/\gamma}]^{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}C\omega\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{(1-\gamma)/\gamma}\cdot\exp(-\alpha\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda}C\omega\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{(1-\gamma)/\gamma}\cdot\frac{\lambda}{\omega}

\displaystyle=C\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{(1-\gamma)/\gamma}

so that, when
\omega/e^{\alpha}\geq\lambda
,

\displaystyle\lambda^{1-c}\left\{1+\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)+\frac{1}{\lambda}\int_{\bar{\nu}^{-1}(\lambda)}^{\infty}\bar{\nu}(t)\,dt\right\}

\displaystyle=\lambda^{1-c}\left[1+\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{1/\gamma}+C\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{(1-\gamma)/\gamma}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\lambda^{1-c}\left[2\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{1/\gamma}+C\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{(1-\gamma)/\gamma}\right]

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\lambda^{1-c}\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{1/\gamma}

since
\gamma>0
implies
1/\gamma>(1-\gamma)/\gamma
. Therefore the overall bound is

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\lambda^{1-c}\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{1/\gamma}\wedge\frac{1}{\lambda^{c}}C\omega m^{1-\gamma}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma})

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}\omega\left(\frac{\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{1/\gamma}}{\lambda^{c-1}}\wedge\frac{m^{1-\gamma}\exp(-\alpha m^{\gamma})}{\lambda^{c}}\right).

∎

Proposition I.5

(Lower bound on effective dimension)
.

Suppose
\nu_{s}\geq\underline{\nu}(s):\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
for some non-increasing positive function
\underline{\nu}(s)
. Then, if
s^{*}
is the smallest positive integer with
\underline{\nu}(s^{*})\leq\lambda
,

\psi(m,2)\geq\int_{s^{*}\vee(m+1)}^{\infty}\frac{\underline{\nu}(s)}{(\underline{\nu}(s)+\lambda)^{2}}\,ds\\
\geq\frac{1}{4\lambda^{2}}\int_{s^{*}\vee(m+1)}^{\infty}\underline{\nu}(s)\,ds.

Moreover, if we have
\underline{\nu}(s)=\omega s^{-\beta}
for
\beta>1
(polynomial decay), then, whenever
\lambda\leq\omega
,

\psi(0,2)\geq\frac{1}{c_{\beta}}\left(\frac{\omega^{1/\beta}}{\lambda^{1+1/\beta}}\right).

If
\underline{\nu}(s)=\omega\exp(-\alpha s^{\gamma})
for
\gamma<1
(exponential decay), then, whenever
\lambda\leq\omega/e^{\alpha}
,

\psi(0,2)\geq\frac{1}{c_{\alpha,\gamma}}\left(\frac{\log(\omega/\lambda)^{(1-\gamma)/\gamma}}{\lambda}\right).

Proof.

We proceed in steps.

1.

Note that the function
t\mapsto(t+\lambda)^{-2}t
is strictly increasing for all
t<\lambda
. Thus, for each integer
s
such that
\underline{\nu}(s)\leq\lambda
we have

\frac{\nu_{s}}{(\nu_{s}+\lambda)^{2}}\geq\int_{s}^{s+1}\frac{\underline{\nu}(t)}{(\underline{\nu}(t)+\lambda)^{2}}\,dt.

So we may bound the sum by an integral to show

\psi(m,2)=\sum_{s=m+1}^{\infty}\frac{\nu_{s}}{(\nu_{s}+\lambda)^{2}}\geq\int_{s^{*}\vee(m+1)}^{\infty}\frac{\underline{\nu}(t)}{(\underline{\nu}(t)+\lambda)^{2}}\,dt\geq\frac{1}{4\lambda^{2}}\int_{s^{*}\vee(m+1)}^{\infty}\underline{\nu}(t)\,dt,

where
s^{*}
is the smallest positive integer such that
\underline{\nu}(s)\leq\lambda
.

2.

Polynomial decay.
If
\underline{\nu}(s)=\omega s^{-\beta}
then
s^{*}\leq(\lambda/\omega)^{-1/\beta}+1
.
Using the exact integral from Proposition
I.2
,

\displaystyle\psi(0,2)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4\lambda^{2}}\int_{s^{*}}^{\infty}\omega s^{-\beta}\,ds\geq\frac{\omega(\beta-1)^{-1}}{4\lambda^{2}}[s^{*}]^{1-\beta}\geq\frac{\omega(\beta-1)^{-1}}{4\lambda^{2}}[(\lambda/\omega)^{-1/\beta}+1]^{1-\beta}.

Under the condition that
\lambda\leq\omega
, the bracketed term is at most
2(\lambda/\omega)^{-1/\beta}
. This yields

\displaystyle\psi(0,2)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{(\beta-1)^{-1}\omega^{1/\beta}}{2^{1+\beta}\lambda^{1+1/\beta}}\geq\frac{1}{c_{\beta,\omega}}\left(\frac{1}{\lambda^{1+1/\beta}}\right).

3.

Exponential decay. If
\underline{\nu}(s)=\omega\exp(-\alpha s^{\gamma})
then we have

\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{1/\gamma}\leq s^{*}\leq\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{1/\gamma}+1\leq 2\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{1/\gamma}

following from the condition that

\lambda\leq\omega/e^{\alpha}
.
We can compute

\displaystyle 4\lambda^{2}\cdot\psi(0,2)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{s^{*}}^{\infty}\omega\exp(-\alpha s^{\gamma})\,ds.

Making the substitution
u=\alpha s^{\gamma}
as in Proposition
I.2
gives us

\displaystyle=\omega\gamma^{-1}\alpha^{\frac{-1}{\gamma}}\int_{\alpha(s^{*})^{\gamma}}^{\infty}\exp(-u)u^{\nicefrac{{(1-\gamma)}}{{\gamma}}}\,du

\displaystyle=\omega\gamma^{-1}\alpha^{\frac{-1}{\gamma}}\Gamma(1/\gamma,\alpha[s^{*}]^{\gamma}).

Using Lemma
I.1
, which says that
\Gamma(z,x)\geq x^{z-1}e^{-x}
for
z>1
, this is

\displaystyle\geq\omega\gamma^{-1}\alpha^{\frac{-1}{\gamma}}(\alpha[s^{*}]^{\gamma})^{1/\gamma-1}e^{-\alpha[s^{*}]^{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\omega\gamma^{-1}\alpha^{-1}(s^{*})^{1-\gamma}\exp(-\alpha[s^{*}]^{\gamma}).

Finally we use the bounds on
s^{*}
in to find that this is

\displaystyle\geq\omega\gamma^{-1}\alpha^{-1}\left[\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{1/\gamma}\right]^{1-\gamma}\exp(-\alpha[2\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{1/\gamma}]^{\gamma})

\displaystyle=\omega\gamma^{-1}\alpha^{-1}\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{(1/\gamma)-1}\frac{\lambda}{\omega}e^{2^{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=\lambda\gamma^{-1}\alpha^{-1}e^{2^{\gamma}}\{\log(\omega/\lambda)/\alpha\}^{(1/\gamma)-1}.

After rearranging, we obtain

\psi(0,2)\geq\frac{1}{c_{\alpha,\gamma}}\left(\frac{\log(\omega/\lambda)^{(1-\gamma)/\gamma}}{\lambda}\right).

∎

Appendix J
Bounding key terms for bootstrap couplings (Section
D
)

The abstract bound is in terms of

\Delta_{1}:=\|\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{HS}}
and
\Delta_{2}:=\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp},

where

\Sigma=\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}],\quad\hat{\Sigma}=\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}.

We bound these key quantities under different assumptions. In particular, we prove
unconditional
bounds, i.e., high probability bounds with respect to
U
.

Assumption J.1

(boundedness)
.

The random variables
U_{i}
satisfy
\left\lVert U_{i}\right\rVert\leq a
almost surely.

Assumption J.2

(sub-Gaussianity)
.

For all
f\in H
, the random variables
U_{i}
satisfy

\mathbb{P}\left\{\left\langle f,\,U_{i}\right\rangle>ub\left(\mathbb{E}\left\langle f,\,U_{i}\right\rangle^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right\}\leq 2e^{-u^{2}}.

J.1
Boundedness

Lemma J.3
.

Under Assumption
J.1
, the following event holds with probability at least
1-2\eta
:

\lVert\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq 2\log(2/\eta)^{2}\left\{\sqrt{\frac{a^{2}\sigma^{2}(0)}{n}}\vee\frac{4a^{2}}{n}\vee\frac{8a^{2}}{n^{2}}\right\}.

Proof.

We proceed in steps.

1.

Decomposition. Write

\displaystyle\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}-\Sigma

\displaystyle=\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\right\}-\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\right\}.

2.

Focusing on the former term, write
\Sigma_{i}=U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}
. Since

\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}=\|U_{i}\|^{2}\leq a^{2}

and

\mathbb{E}\|\Sigma_{i}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{HS}}=\int\tr(\Sigma_{i}^{*}\Sigma_{i})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\leq\int\|\Sigma_{i}\|_{op}\tr(\Sigma_{i})\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}\leq a^{2}\tr(\Sigma)=a^{2}\sigma^{2}(0),

by Lemma
H.2
, with probability
1-\eta
,

\lVert\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq 2\log(2/\eta)\left\{\sqrt{\frac{a^{2}\sigma^{2}(0)}{n}}\vee\frac{2a^{2}}{n}\right\}.

3.

Focusing on the latter term, write

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\|^{2}=\|\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]-0\|^{2}.

Notice that

\|U_{i}\|\leq a
and
\mathbb{E}\|U_{i}\|^{2}\leq a^{2}

so by Lemma
H.2
, with probability
1-\eta
,

\|\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]-0\|\leq 2\log(2/\eta)\left\{\sqrt{\frac{a^{2}}{n}}\vee\frac{2a}{n}\right\}

and therefore

\|\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq 4\log(2/\eta)^{2}\left\{\frac{a^{2}}{n}\vee\frac{4a^{2}}{n^{2}}\right\}=2\log(2/\eta)^{2}\left\{\frac{2a^{2}}{n}\vee\frac{8a^{2}}{n^{2}}\right\}.

4.

In summary

\displaystyle\lVert\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}+\|\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\log(2/\eta)\left\{\sqrt{\frac{a^{2}\sigma^{2}(0)}{n}}\vee\frac{2a^{2}}{n}\right\}+2\log(2/\eta)^{2}\left\{\frac{2a^{2}}{n}\vee\frac{8a^{2}}{n^{2}}\right\}.

∎

Lemma J.4
.

Under Assumption
J.1
, the following event holds with probability at least
1-\eta
:

\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\leq 2\log(2/\eta)\left(\sqrt{\frac{a^{2}\sigma^{2}(m)}{n}}\vee\frac{2a^{2}}{n}\right).

Proof.

We proceed in steps.

1.

Decomposition. As before

\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp}=\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}-\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}.

2.

Focusing on the former term, notice that

\tr[\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}]=\mathbb{E}_{n}\xi_{i}-\mathbb{E}\xi_{i},\quad\xi_{i}=\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma_{i}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\xi_{i}

\displaystyle=\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma_{i}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}=\tr\{\Pi_{m}^{\perp}U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\}=\|\Pi_{m}^{\perp}U_{i}\|^{2}\leq\|U_{i}\|^{2}\leq a^{2},

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq a^{2}\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}=a^{2}\int\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma_{i}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}=a^{2}\int\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma_{i}\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}=a^{2}\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma=a^{2}\sigma^{2}(m),

so by Lemma
H.2
, with probability
1-\eta
,

\tr[\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}]\leq 2\log(2/\eta)\left\{\sqrt{\frac{a^{2}\sigma^{2}(m)}{n}}\vee\frac{2a^{2}}{n}\right\}.

3.

Focusing on the latter term, notice that

\tr[\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}]=\|\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\|^{2}\geq 0.

4.

In summary

\displaystyle\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\tr[\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}]

\displaystyle\qquad-\tr[\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}]

\displaystyle\leq\tr[\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}]

\displaystyle\leq 2\log(2/\eta)\left\{\sqrt{\frac{a^{2}\sigma^{2}(m)}{n}}\vee\frac{2a^{2}}{n}\right\}.

∎

J.2
Sub-Gaussianity

The sub-Gaussian case is more involved. We quote some helpful results before bounding the key terms.

J.2.1
Preliminaries

Lemma J.5

(
Dirksen, 2015
)
.

Let
(X_{t})_{t\in T}
denote a real-valued stochastic process on the separable metric space
(T,d)
, whose increments are sub-Gaussian with respect to
d
, i.e.

\mathbb{P}\left(\vphantom{\sum}|X_{s}-X_{t}|>ud(s,t)\right)\leq 2e^{-u^{2}}.
Define the
\gamma
functional

\gamma(T,d)=\inf_{S}\sup_{t\in T}\;\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}2^{n/2}d(S_{n},t),

where the infimum is over sequences
S=(S_{0},S_{1},\ldots)
of subsets of
T
satisfying the growth condition
\#S_{0}=1
,
\#S_{n}\leq 2^{2^{n}}
, and
d(S_{n},t)\coloneqq\inf_{s\in S_{n}}d(s,t)
. Then, there exist universal constants
C,D
such that the following inequalities hold, for any
t_{0}\in T
:

\left(\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in T}|X_{t}-X_{t_{0}}|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C\gamma(T,d)+2\sup_{t\in T}\left(\mathbb{E}|X_{t}-X_{t_{0}}|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Lemma J.6

(
Talagrand, 2014
)
.

Let
(Z_{t})_{t\in T}
be a Gaussian process on the separable metric space
(T,d)
, where
d(s,t)=\left(\mathbb{E}|Z_{s}-Z_{t}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}
. Then there exists a universal constant
C>0
such that

C^{-1}\gamma(T,d)\leq\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in T}|Z_{t}|\leq C\gamma(T,d).

J.2.2
Bounding moments

Lemma J.7
.

Under Assumption
J.2
, there exists a universal constant
C
such that

\left(\vphantom{\sum}\mathbb{E}\left\lVert U_{i}\right\rVert^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq Cb\sqrt{p}\sigma(0).

Proof.

We proceed in steps.

1.

Matching symbols. We consider the process
X_{t}=\left\langle U_{i},\,t\right\rangle
indexed by
t\in B_{H}
. Note in particular that if
d(s,t)=\left\langle(s-t),\,b^{2}C(s-t)\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}
then
(X_{t})_{t\in B_{H}}
satisfies the sub-Gaussian increments condition with respect to
d
:

\mathbb{P}\left(\left\langle s-t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle>ub\left(\mathbb{E}\left\langle s-t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\leq 2e^{-u^{2}}

implies

\mathbb{P}\left(\vphantom{\sum}|X_{s}-X_{t}|>ud(s,t)\right)\leq 2e^{-u^{2}}

when

d(s,t)=b\left(\mathbb{E}\left\langle s-t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\left\langle(s-t),\,b^{2}C(s-t)\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Note also that
\sup_{t\in B_{H}}|X_{t}|=\sup_{t\in B}\left\langle U_{i},\,t\right\rangle=\left\lVert U_{i}\right\rVert.
Applying Lemma
J.5
to
(X_{t})_{t\in B_{H}}
with
t_{0}=0
therefore yields

\left(\mathbb{E}\left\lVert U_{i}\right\rVert^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C\gamma(B_{H},d)+2\sup_{t\in B_{H}}\left(\mathbb{E}|\left\langle t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

2.

First term. To bound
\gamma(B_{H},d)
, note that if
Z_{t}=b\langle\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g,\,t\rangle
so that
\mathbb{E}|Z_{s}-Z_{t}|^{2}=d(s,t)^{2}
by construction, it holds by Lemma
J.6
that

\displaystyle\gamma(B_{H},d)

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{E}\sup_{t\in B_{H}}|b\langle\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g,\,t\rangle|=bC\mathbb{E}\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g\rVert\leq bC\left(\mathbb{E}\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g\rVert^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=bC\sigma(0).

In particular

\mathbb{E}\lVert\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g\rVert^{2}=\mathbb{E}[\tr g^{*}\Sigma g]=\mathbb{E}[\tr\Sigma g\otimes g^{*}]=\tr\Sigma=\sigma^{2}(0).

3.

Second term. What remains is control of the latter term
\sup_{t\in B_{H}}\left(\mathbb{E}|\left\langle t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}
. Initially fix
t
. To begin, we argue that
\left\langle t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle
is sub-Gaussian with
\left\lVert\left\langle U_{i},\,t\right\rangle\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}}\leq Cb\left\langle t,\,\Sigma t\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}
. To see why, notice that in our sub-Gaussian assumption,

b\left(\mathbb{E}\left\langle t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=b\left\langle t,\,\Sigma t\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Therefore we are effectively assuming

\left\|\frac{\left\langle t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle}{b\left\langle t,\,\Sigma t\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}}\right\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq C\iff\left\|\left\langle t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle\right\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq Cb\left\langle t,\,\Sigma t\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Since
\left\langle t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle
is sub-Gaussian, we can appeal to
Vershynin, 2018
to control its moments. In particular,

\left(\mathbb{E}|\left\langle t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq C\sqrt{p}\|\left\langle t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq Cb\sqrt{p}\left\langle t,\,\Sigma t\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Taking the supremum over
t\in B_{H}
,

\sup_{t\in B_{H}}\left(\mathbb{E}|\left\langle t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq Cb\sqrt{p}\sup_{t\in B_{H}}\left\langle t,\,\Sigma t\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}=Cb\sqrt{p}\nu_{1}^{1/2}\leq Cb\sqrt{p}\sigma(0).

4.

Collecting results. In summary,

\displaystyle\left(\mathbb{E}\left\lVert U_{i}\right\rVert^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\leq C\gamma(B_{H},d)+2\sup_{t\in B_{H}}\left(\mathbb{E}|\left\langle t,\,U_{i}\right\rangle|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq bC\sigma(0)+Cb\sqrt{p}\sigma(0)=Cb\sqrt{p}\sigma(0).

∎

Lemma J.8
.

Under Assumption
J.2
, there exists a universal constant
C
such that

\left(\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\Pi_{m}^{\perp}U_{i}\right\rVert^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\leq Cb\sqrt{p}\sigma(m).

Proof.

We use as identical proof to Lemma
J.7
, replacing
U_{i}
with
\Pi_{m}^{\perp}U_{i}
. In particular, we consider the process

X_{t}=\left\langle\Pi_{m}^{\perp}U_{i},\,t\right\rangle=\left\langle U_{i},\,\Pi_{m}^{\perp}t\right\rangle
which corresponds restricting the index set to
t\in\Pi_{m}^{\perp}B_{H}
. Since this process is simply a restriction of the original process, the increment condition is again satisfied, and it suffices to plug in the improved bounds

\displaystyle\gamma(\Pi_{m}^{\perp}B_{H},d)

\displaystyle\leq bC\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma^{\frac{1}{2}}g\right\rVert\leq bC\sigma^{2}(m),

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in\Pi_{m}^{\perp}B_{H}}\left\lVert\left\langle U_{i},\,t\right\rangle\right\rVert_{\psi_{2}}

\displaystyle\leq Cb\sup_{t\in B_{H}}\left\langle\Pi_{m}^{\perp}t,\,\Sigma\Pi_{m}^{\perp}t\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}}=Cb\sqrt{\nu_{m}}\leq b\sigma(m).

∎

J.2.3
Main result

Lemma J.9
.

Under Assumption
J.2
, the following event holds with probability at least
1-2\eta
,

\lVert\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq C\log(2/\eta)^{2}\frac{b^{2}\sigma^{2}(0)}{\sqrt{n}}.

Proof.

We proceed in steps.

1.

Decomposition. As before, write

\displaystyle\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}-\Sigma

\displaystyle=\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\right\}-\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\right\}.

2.

Focusing on the first term, write
\Sigma_{i}=U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}
. Notice that

\displaystyle\|\|\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\|_{p}

\displaystyle\leq\|\|\Sigma_{i}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}+\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\|_{p}\leq\|\|\Sigma_{i}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\|_{p}+\|\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\|_{p}.

Note that the former term is

\|\|\Sigma_{i}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\|^{p}_{p}=\mathbb{E}(\|\Sigma_{i}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}^{p}).

Within the latter term

\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{HS}}=\|\mathbb{E}(\Sigma_{i})\|_{HS}\leq\mathbb{E}(\|\Sigma_{i}\|_{\mathrm{HS}})

hence

\|\|\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\|^{p}_{p}\leq\mathbb{E}[\{\mathbb{E}(\|\Sigma_{i}\|_{\mathrm{HS}})\}^{p}]\leq\mathbb{E}\{\mathbb{E}(\|\Sigma_{i}\|^{p}_{\mathrm{HS}})\}=\mathbb{E}(\|\Sigma_{i}\|^{p}_{\mathrm{HS}}).

In summary

\|\|\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\|_{p}\leq 2\|\|\Sigma_{i}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\|_{p}.

Finally, we have

\|\Sigma_{i}\|^{p}_{\mathrm{HS}}=\|U_{i}\|^{2p}.

Thus, we can estimate using Lemma
J.7
that

\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma\right\rVert^{p}_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq 2^{p}\mathbb{E}(\left\lVert U_{i}\right\rVert^{2p})\leq(2b^{2}C^{2})^{p}(\sqrt{2p})^{2p}\sigma^{2p}(0)

since

\mathbb{E}\left\lVert U_{i}\right\rVert^{q}\leq\{Cb\sqrt{q}\sigma(0)\}^{q}
where
q=2p.

3.

By Stirling’s approximation, we have

p!\geq(p/e)^{p}
, so
(2e)^{p}p!\geq(2p)^{p}=(\sqrt{2p})^{2p}
.
Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma\right\rVert^{p}_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle\leq(2b^{2}C^{2})^{p}(2e)^{p}p!\sigma^{2p}(0)=(2^{1/p}4eb^{2}C^{2})^{p}\frac{1}{2}p!\sigma^{2p}(0)=(C^{\prime\prime})^{p}\frac{1}{2}p!\sigma^{2p}(0)

and therefore

\mathbb{E}\left\lVert\frac{\Sigma_{i}-\Sigma}{C^{\prime\prime}}\right\rVert^{p}_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq\frac{1}{2}p!\{\sigma^{2}(0)\}^{p}
where
C^{\prime\prime}=2^{1/p}4eb^{2}C^{2}.

Therefore by applying Lemma
H.2
with
A=B=\sigma^{2}(0)
, we see that with probability at least
1-\eta
,

\displaystyle\left\lVert\mathbb{E}_{n}[\Sigma_{i}]-\Sigma\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle=C^{\prime\prime}\left\lVert\frac{\mathbb{E}_{n}[\Sigma_{i}]-\Sigma}{C^{\prime\prime}}\right\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime}2\ln(2/\eta)\left\{\frac{\sigma^{2}(0)}{n}\vee\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{4}(0)}{n}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 2C^{\prime\prime}\ln(2/\eta)\frac{\sigma^{2}(0)}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\leq 16eb^{2}C^{2}\ln(2/\eta)\frac{\sigma^{2}(0)}{\sqrt{n}}.

4.

Turning to the second term, write

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle=\|\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\|^{2}=\|\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]-0\|^{2}.

By Lemma
J.7
,

\mathbb{E}\|U_{i}\|^{p}\leq\{Cb\sqrt{p}\sigma(0)\}^{p}=(bC)^{p}\sqrt{p}^{p}\{\sigma(0)\}^{p}.

By Stirling’s approximation,
p!\geq(p/e)^{p}
so
\sqrt{p}^{p}\leq(e^{p}p!)^{1/2}\leq e^{p/2}p!
. Therefore

\mathbb{E}\|U_{i}\|^{p}\leq(bCe^{1/2})^{p}p!\{\sigma(0)\}^{p}=(C^{\prime})^{p}\frac{1}{2}p!\{\sigma(0)\}^{p},\quad C^{\prime}=2^{1/p}bCe^{1/2}.

Applying Lemma
H.2
with
A=B=\sigma(0)
, with probability at least
1-\eta
,

\displaystyle\|\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\|

\displaystyle=C^{\prime}\left\|\frac{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]}{C^{\prime}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime}2\ln(2/\eta)\left\{\frac{\sigma(0)}{n}\vee\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{2}(0)}{n}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 2C^{\prime}\ln(2/\eta)\frac{\sigma(0)}{\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\leq 4bCe^{1/2}\ln(2/\eta)\frac{\sigma(0)}{\sqrt{n}}.

Therefore

\|\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}\leq 16b^{2}C^{2}e\ln(2/\eta)^{2}\frac{\sigma^{2}(0)}{n}.

5.

In summary

\displaystyle\lVert\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle\leq\lVert\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\rVert_{\mathrm{HS}}+\|\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\|_{\mathrm{HS}}

\displaystyle\leq 16eb^{2}C^{2}\ln(2/\eta)\frac{\sigma^{2}(0)}{\sqrt{n}}+16b^{2}C^{2}e\ln(2/\eta)^{2}\frac{\sigma^{2}(0)}{n}.

∎

Lemma J.10
.

Under Assumption
J.2
, the following event holds with probability at least
1-\eta
:

\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\leq C\log(2/\eta)\frac{b^{2}\sigma^{2}(m)}{\sqrt{n}}.

Proof.

We proceed in steps.

1.

Decomposition. As before

\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp}=\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}-\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}.

2.

Focusing on the first term, notice that

\tr[\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}]=\mathbb{E}_{n}\xi_{i}-\mathbb{E}\xi_{i},\quad\xi_{i}=\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma_{i}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}.

Moreover

\|\xi_{i}-\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}\|_{p}\leq\|\xi_{i}\|_{p}+\|\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}\|_{p}.

The former term is

\|\xi_{i}\|^{p}_{p}=\mathbb{E}(|\xi_{i}|^{p}).

Within the latter term

\|\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}\|^{p}_{p}=\mathbb{E}[|\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}|^{p}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}(|\xi_{i}|^{p})]=\mathbb{E}(|\xi_{i}|^{p}).

In summary

\|\xi_{i}-\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}\|_{p}\leq 2\|\xi_{i}\|_{p}.

Then

\xi_{i}=\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\Sigma_{i}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}=\tr\{\Pi_{m}^{\perp}U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\}=\|\Pi_{m}^{\perp}U_{i}\|^{2}.

By Lemma
J.8
,

\mathbb{E}(|\xi_{i}-\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}|^{p})\leq 2^{p}\mathbb{E}(|\xi_{i}|^{p})=2^{p}\mathbb{E}(\|\Pi_{m}^{\perp}U_{i}\|^{2p})\leq 2^{p}\{Cb\sqrt{2p}\sigma(m)\}^{2p}.

3.

By Stirling’s approximation,
p!\geq(p/e)^{p}
, so
(2e)^{p}p!\geq(2p)^{p}=(\sqrt{2p})^{2p}
. Hence

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(|\xi_{i}|^{p})

\displaystyle\leq 2^{p}\{Cb\}^{2p}(2e)^{p}p!\{\sigma^{2}(m)\}^{p}

\displaystyle=(2^{1/p}4eb^{2}C^{2})^{p}\frac{1}{2}p!\{\sigma^{2}(m)\}^{p}

\displaystyle=(C^{\prime\prime\prime})^{p}\frac{1}{2}p!\{\sigma^{2}(m)\}^{p}

and therefore

\mathbb{E}\left(\left|\frac{\xi_{i}}{C^{\prime\prime\prime}}\right|^{p}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}p!\{\sigma^{2}(m)\}^{p},\quad C^{\prime\prime\prime}=2^{1/p}4eb^{2}C^{2}.

Applying Lemma
H.2
with
A=B=\sigma^{2}(m)
, we see that with probability
1-\eta

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{n}\xi_{i}-\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}|

\displaystyle=C^{\prime\prime\prime}\left|\frac{\mathbb{E}_{n}\xi_{i}-\mathbb{E}\xi_{i}}{C^{\prime\prime\prime}}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime\prime}2\ln(2/\eta)\left\{\frac{\sigma^{2}(m)}{n}\vee\sqrt{\frac{\sigma^{4}(m)}{n}}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 2C^{\prime\prime\prime}\ln(2/\eta)\frac{\sigma^{2}(m)}{\sqrt{n}}.

\displaystyle\leq 16eb^{2}C^{2}\ln(2/\eta)\frac{\sigma^{2}(m)}{\sqrt{n}}.

4.

Focusing on the second term, notice that

\tr[\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}]=\|\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\|^{2}\geq 0.

5.

In summary

\displaystyle\tr\Pi_{m}^{\perp}(\hat{\Sigma}-\Sigma)\Pi_{m}^{\perp}

\displaystyle=\tr[\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}]

\displaystyle\qquad-\tr[\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}]\otimes(\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}])^{*}\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}]

\displaystyle\leq\tr[\Pi_{m}^{\perp}\left\{\mathbb{E}_{n}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]-\mathbb{E}[U_{i}\otimes U_{i}^{*}]\right\}\Pi_{m}^{\perp}]

\displaystyle\leq 16eb^{2}C^{2}\ln(2/\eta)\frac{\sigma^{2}(m)}{\sqrt{n}}.

∎

K.2
Pseudo true coverage under mis-specification

In this appendix, we present additional results for the mis-specified Gaussian kernel. While coverage for the true parameter
f_{0}\not\in H
breaks down, coverage for the pseudo true parameter
f_{\lambda}\in H
remains nominal across sample sizes and regularization values. These simulations showcase how our inferential theory delivers meaningful guarantees without any assumptions on the bias
f_{\lambda}-f_{0}
. Again, we document additional metrics such as bias and width of the confidence bands.

K.3
Implementation details

In Section
4.2
, each observation is generated as follows. Draw
X_{i}\sim\mathrm{Unif}([0,1])
and
\varepsilon_{i}\sim\mathrm{Unif}([-2,2])
independently for each
1\leq i\leq n
. Then set
Y_{i}=f_{0}(X_{i})+\varepsilon_{i}
where

f_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{5}}\sum_{i=1}^{5}e_{i}g_{i},

where
e_{i}
denote the eigenfunctions of the integral operator
\mathbb{E}[k_{X_{i}}k_{X_{i}}^{*}]
, and the
g_{i}
are independent standard normal multipliers. We implement Estimator
4.1
with the Gaussian kernel
k(x,y)=\exp\{-\lVert x-y\rVert/(2\iota^{2})\}
, with the scale
\iota
set to
0.1
.

In Section
4.3
, each observation is generated as in section
4.2
, with the following changes. The covariates
X_{i}
are a uniform random permutation of
\left\{1,2,\cdots,7\right\}
, and the kernel
k
is chosen to be the Mallows kernel
k(\pi,\pi^{\prime})=\exp\{-\tau(\pi,\pi^{\prime})/(2\iota^{2})\}
; here
\tau
is Kendall’s-
\tau
function which counts the number of discordant pairs. We employ the standard heuristic and choose
\iota
to be the median of the
\tau(X_{i},X_{j})
for independent draws of the data.

In Section
K.1
, each observation is generated as in Section
4.2
, replacing
f_{0}
with the third eigenfunction of the Matern kernel. We implement Estimator
4.1
with the length-scale parameter set to
0.1
.

In Section
K.2
, each observation is generated as in Section
4.2
, replacing
f_{0}
with the step function
f_{0}(x)=\mathbbm{1}\{x\geq 1/2\}
. We implement Estimator
4.1
, with the length-scale parameter set to
0.1
.

Appendix L
Application details

L.1
Random utility model

In order to evaluate our proposed direct approach, we generate synthetic data which resemble real student preferences using the discrete choice model estimated and reported in
Pathak and Shi, 2021
.
In that paper, the authors fit several common discrete choice models to students’ reported preference data from the Boston Public Schools centralized match, and then assess each model’s ability to forecast demand. As Boston Public Schools used a student-proposing variant of the deferred acceptance (DA) algorithm of
Gale and Shapley, 1962
, strategy-proofness holds and students can reasonably be assumed to report preferences truthfully
(
Pathak and Sönmez, 2008
)
.

In the random utility model of
Pathak and Shi, 2021
, the utility of student
i
at school
s
is

u_{is}=\hat{}\alpha_{s}+\hat{\beta}_{1}^{\top}X_{1}+\beta_{2}^{\top}X_{2}+\epsilon_{i}.

In this model,
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are student-school covariates such as student race, school demographics, walking distance to the school, student English language learner status ,and the availability of English instructional programs in the student’s language. The vector
\hat{\beta}_{1}
contains estimated coefficients, while
\beta_{2}\sim N(\hat{\mu}_{2},\hat{\Sigma}_{2})
are random coefficients fit using the mixed multinomial logit procedure outlined in the paper. Finally,
\hat{}\alpha_{s}
is a school fixed-effect, and
\epsilon_{i}
is an independent standard Gumbel random variable.

For the scenario of no match effects, we generate student preferences as described above. We draw school effects as

Y_{is}=\mu+\sigma\delta_{i}+\gamma_{1}P_{s},

where
\delta_{i}
is a standard normal random variable. We truncate
Y_{is}
to lie in
[0,100]
.

For the scenario of match effects, we generate student preferences with an additional taste parameter:

u_{is}=\hat{}\alpha_{s}+\hat{\beta}_{1}^{\top}X_{1}+\beta_{2}^{\top}X_{2}+\chi_{i}P_{s}\epsilon_{i},

where
P_{s}
indicated whether
s
is a pilot school and
\chi_{i}
represents preference for pilot sector schools. Here,
\chi_{i}
determines sector effects: we generate counterfactual outcomes by calculating and then truncating

Y_{is}=\mu+\sigma\delta_{i}+\gamma\chi_{i}P_{s},

so students who prefer pilot sector schools also benefit more from them.

L.2
Comparison to propensity score conditioning

As remarked in the main text, group average treatment effects for rank strata (and, indeed, individual preference types) are also identified by simple averages. However, when the strata are relatively small, the resulting measurements are far too noisy for meaningful statistical inference. KRR improves precision by using the full ranked list to predict outcomes. To illustrate this point, we compare our methodology using KRR to the standard inverse propensity-weighted averages below.

(a)

KRR (uniform bands)

(b)

IPW (uniform bands)

(c)

IPW (pointwise bands)

Figure 12
:
KRR inference improves power. In our experiment, simply averaging the inverse propensity-weighted score frequently produces the incorrect sign (with pointwise signifiance), and uniform confidence bands are uninformative.
